Stochastic interpretations of the oceanic primitive equations with relaxed hydrostatic assumptions
Arnaud Debussche, Étienne Mémin, Antoine Moneyron

Abstract
In this paper, we investigate how weakening the classical hydrostatic balance hypothesis impacts the well-posedness of the stochastic LU primitive equations. The models we consider are intermediate between the incompressible 3D LU Navier-Stokes equations and the LU primitive equations with standard hydrostatic balance. As such, they are expected to be numerically tractable, while accounting well for phenomena within the grey zone between hydrostatic balance and non-hydrostatic processes. Our main result is the well-posedness of a low-pass filtering-based stochastic interpretation of the LU primitive equations, with rigid-lid type boundary conditions, in the limit of “quasi-barotropic” flow. This assumption is linked to the structure assumption proposed in [ 1 , 2 ] , which can be related to the dynamical regime where the primitive equations remain valid [ 40 ] . Furthermore, we present and study two eddy-(hyper)viscosity-based models.



1
Introduction

Stochastic modelling for large-scale fluid flows and their dynamics representation is nowadays a key research topic. Geophysical flows cannot be numerically represented in their full complexity, since they are characterised by a fully developed turbulence and chaotic dynamical systems. Therefore, only approximated large-scale models can be computationally considered. In the last years, stochastic modelling has emerged as a powerful setting for such representation
[
6
,
26
,
27
,
39
]
. In particular, climate change studies call for models able to handle approximations or errors together with their time evolution. The need for plausible realisations in probabilistic forecasting requires carrying out a set of physically relevant realisations, with efficient uncertainty quantification properties. Stochastic modelling and stochastic calculus offer practical methodological frameworks to deal with these issues. The first models of this type were based on phenomenological turbulence studies on backscattering energy across scales
[
36
,
41
]
, and typically involve multiplicative random forcing models or stochastic parametrisation
[
9
,
52
]
. However, the noise variance being uncontrolled
a priori
, an eddy viscosity was added to balance the noise energy. The precise form of this additional viscosity is still unknown, but it often relies on the debatable Boussinesq turbulence assumption in practice
[
51
]
. Also, random forcing defined outside of any conservation principle may lead to a poor accuracy compared to the reference fine deterministic resolution
[
13
]
, and to a lack of interpretability.

During the past decade, the location uncertainty approach (LU) has been developed and studied to propose physically consistent stochastic models
[
43
,
56
]
. This approach relies on a stochastic version of the Reynolds transport theorem, applied to the mass, momentum and energy conservation
[
43
]
. The LU formalism has been successfully applied to classical geophysical models
[
4
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
, stochastic reduced order models
[
49
,
50
,
55
]
and large eddy simulation models
[
12
,
11
,
32
]
. Its physical relevance has also been tested on prototypical flow models
[
4
,
7
,
13
]
. Additionally, in a recent paper
[
19
]
, the authors demonstrated that the stochastic version of the 2D Navier-Stokes equation under location uncertainty is well-posed, and that the 3D one admits a martingale solution. This is consistent with the results in the deterministic setting, as the well-posedness of the classical 3D Navier-Stokes equation remains an open problem. Another important result was the continuity of the stochastic model for a vanishing noise, which shows the consistency of the stochastic model compared to the associated deterministic one. The noise considered in the LU setting corresponds to the so-called transport noise. This type of noise has been subject to intensive research efforts in the mathematics community due to the need for well-posedness properties for fluid dynamics models
[
1
,
2
,
8
,
15
,
19
,
20
,
23
,
24
,
29
,
35
,
44
]
, and because of the emergence of enhanced dissipation and mixing
[
21
,
25
]
.

In the deterministic setting, the primitive equations are commonly used to model geophysical flows
[
57
]
. They are derived from the 3D Navier-Stokes equations, making the hypothesis whereby the vertical acceleration is negligible. This leads to the classical hydrostatic equilibrium on the vertical component, which relates the vertical derivative of the pressure to the density fluctuation. This balance is known to be physically valid in the ocean at large-scale. However, it breaks down outside of the shallow water regime, or when thermodynamic effects take place, such as deep convection. We call this assumption the
strong hydrostatic hypothesis
. Remarkably, this deterministic model is known to be well-posed
[
10
]
under suitable boundary conditions – namely the rigid-lid ones – the proof relying essentially on the study of barotropic and baroclinic velocity modes. Various authors have studied stochastic versions of these equations. The well-posedness of the stochastic primitive equations with multiplicative noise has been shown in
[
17
,
18
]
, and more recently with a specific class of regular transport noise in
[
8
]
. Moreover, in a recent paper, it has been shown that the stochastic primitive equations with transport noise that is similar to the LU one are well-posed, under the strong hydrostatic hypothesis and using periodic channel-type boundary conditions
[
1
]
. However, the horizontal noise was assumed to be independent of the vertical axis in this work, which makes the barotropic and baroclinic noises tractable.

As pointed out previously, the strong hydrostatic balance does not allow to represent processes with a non-negligible vertical acceleration such as deep convection phenomena, which exhibit strong up- or down-welling of water. Yet, modelling these phenomena is crucial to represent faithfully thermohaline circulation and deep currents – such as the Atlantic meridional overturning circulation (AMOC) – in the long-run and at coarse spatial resolution (i.e. at the climatic scale). The LU setting allows to relax easily the strong hydrostatic balance by considering the martingale terms of the vertical acceleration as deviation terms. This immediately yields a generalization of the primitive equations. Therefore, in this paper, we study the well-posedness of the LU primitive equations under a weaker hydrostatic equilibrium assumption: we account for the transport of the vertical velocity by the noise. However, the choice of the shape for the energy compensation dramatically impacts the theoretical properties of the system. This leads to two potential models of interest. Such models are intermediate between the stochastic primitive equations under strong hydrostatic hypothesis and the 3D Navier-Stokes equations – with Boussinesq’s assumption of weak compressibility. Also, they are expected to be numerically tractable, while accounting better for non-hydrostatic phenomena, such as solitons, internal waves or oceanic convection, to name but a few.

The paper is organised as follows. First, we introduce the LU framework and precise our assumptions to derive a suitable class of LU representations for the primitive equations. Then we describe the functional setting of this study and state the main results of the paper. After this, we show the existence of a global martingale solution for a class of models with low-pass filtered noise. Then we show that there exists a unique local pathwise solution for such models. Considering an approximation of this low-pass filtered model in the limit of “quasi-barotropic” flow, and improving the regularity of the filtering kernel, we prove eventually that there exists a unique global-in-time pathwise solution. Additionally, we show that the solution to this latter model is continuous with respect to the initial data and to the noise data in a well-chosen topology. Moreover, we present two non-filtered (hyper)diffusive models, for which there exist global martingale solutions. Sketches of proofs for their
L^{2}
-energy estimates are given in the appendix, the rest of the proofs being similar to one for the filtered model.

2
Primitive equations models in the LU framework

The LU formulation is based on the following time-scale separation of the flow:

dX_{t}=u(X_{t},t)dt+\sigma(X_{t},t)dW_{t}.

(2.1)

In this decomposition, which should be understood in the Itō sense,
X
denotes the Lagrangian displacement defined in a bounded cylindrical tridimensional domain
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{H}\times[-h,0]\subset\mathbbm{R}^{3}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{H}
is a subset of
\mathbbm{R}^{2}
with smooth boundary. This formulation corresponds to a flat bottom assumption, even if we expect the results presented in this paper to be similar for a smooth enough non-flat bottom. The velocity component
u(X_{t},t)
denotes the large-scale Eulerian velocity (correlated in both space and time) and
\sigma(X_{t},t)dW
is a highly oscillating unresolved velocity (uncorrelated in time but correlated in space). We further interpret this second component as a noise term.

Let us define this noise term more precisely: consider a Wiener process
W
on the space of square integrable functions
\mathcal{W}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})
. Thus, there exists a Hilbert orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}
of
\mathcal{W}
and a sequence of independent standard Brownian motions
(\hat{\beta}^{i})_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}
on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t},\mathbbm{P})
such that,

W=\sum_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}\hat{\beta}^{i}e_{i}.

As such,
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t},\mathbbm{P},W)
is a stochastic basis. Note that the sum
\sum_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}\hat{\beta}^{i}e_{i}
does
not
converge in
\mathcal{W}
. Hence, the previous identity only makes sense in a space
\mathcal{U}
including
\mathcal{W}
, such that the embedding
\mathcal{W}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. For instance,
\mathcal{U}
can be the dual space of any reproducing kernel Hilbert subspace
of
\mathcal{W}
for the inner product
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{\mathcal{W}}
, e.g.
H^{-s}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})
with
s>\frac{3}{2}
. Then, we define the noise through a deterministic time dependent correlation operator
\sigma_{t}
: let
\hat{\sigma}:[0,T]\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{2},\mathbbm{R}^{3})
be a bounded symmetric kernel and define

(\sigma_{t}f)(x)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\hat{\sigma}(x,y,t)f(y)dy,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall f\in\mathcal{W}.

With this definition,
\sigma_{t}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator mapping
\mathcal{W}
into itself, so that the noise can be written as

\sigma_{t}W_{t}=\sum_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}\hat{\beta}_{t}^{i}\sigma_{t}e_{i},

as this series converges in
\mathcal{W}
almost surely, and in
L^{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{W})
for all
p\in\mathbbm{N}
. Here we interpret
\mathcal{W}
as the space carrying the Wiener process
W_{t}
, while the notation
L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})
is kept for denoting the space of tridimensional velocities. Moreover, there exists Hilbert basis
(\phi_{n})_{n}
consisting of eigenfunctions of the noise operator
\sigma_{t}
. Here, we scale these eigenfunctions by their corresponding eigenvalues. By a change of basis, there also exists a sequence of standard Brownian motions
(\beta_{t}^{k})_{k}
, defined on the same filtered space, such that

\sigma_{t}W_{t}=\sum_{i\in\mathbbm{N}}\beta_{t}^{k}\phi_{k}.

Furthermore, we can associate a covariance tensor to the random field
\sigma W_{t}
: if
x,y\in\mathcal{S}
are two space points, and
t,s\in\mathbbm{R}^{+}
are two time points, define
Q
formally by

Q(x,y,t,s)=\mathbbm{E}[(\sigma_{t}dW_{t})(x)(\sigma_{s}dW_{s})(y)]=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\delta(t-s)\hat{\sigma}(x,z,t)\hat{\sigma}(z,y,s)dz.

The diagonal part of this covariance tensor is referred to as the variance tensor, and is denoted

a(x,t)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\hat{\sigma}(x,y,t)\hat{\sigma}(y,x,t)dy=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}(x,t)\phi_{k}(x,t)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\in\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3}.

(2.2)

Moreover, the variance tensor
a
is assumed to fulfil
a\in L^{1}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3}))
, which will be enforced further by the regularity conditions on
\sigma
– see equation (
3.1
). Let us note that, in full generality – that is when
\hat{\sigma}_{t}
is itself a random function – the random-matrix process
a
is subject to an integrability condition,

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|a(\boldsymbol{\cdot},t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}<\infty,

where
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})}
is the Hilbert norm associated to
L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})
, the matrix space
\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3}
being equipped with the Frobenius norm. As such, the integral
\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}
is a
\mathcal{W}
-valued Gaussian process with expectation zero and bounded variance:
\mathbbm{E}\big[\|\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big]<\infty
. The quadratic variation of
\int_{0}^{t}(\sigma_{t}dW_{s})(x)
is given by the bounded variation process
\int_{0}^{t}a(x,s)ds.

Similarly to the classical derivation of the Navier-Stokes equations, we may derive the LU Navier-Stokes equations using a stochastic version of the Reynolds Transport Theorem (SRTT)
[
43
]
. Let
q
be a random scalar, within a volume
\mathcal{V}(t)
transported by the flow. Then, for incompressible unresolved flows – that is
\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sigma_{t}=0
– the SRTT reads

\displaystyle d\Big(\int_{\mathcal{V}(t)}q(x,t)dx\Big)=\int_{\mathcal{V}(t)}\big(\mathbbm{D}_{t}q+q\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(u-u_{s})dt\big)dx,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}q=d_{t}q+(u-u_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla q\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla q-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla q)dt,

(2.4)

where an additional drift
u_{s}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}a
, coined as the Itō-Stokes drift in
[
4
]
, is involved. Here,
d_{t}q(x,t)=q(x,t+dt)-q(x,t)
is the forward time increment at a fixed spatial point
x
, and
\mathbbm{D}_{t}q
is a stochastic transport operator introduced in
[
43
,
46
]
, which plays the role of the material derivative. The Itō-Stokes drift is directly related to the divergence of the variance tensor
a
, which represents the effects of noise inhomogeneity on the large-scale dynamics. Such advection terms are commonly added as corrective terms in ocean large-scale simulation to account for surface waves and Langmuir turbulence
[
14
,
30
,
42
]
. As shown in
[
4
]
, the LU framework naturally exhibits similar features, generalizing the effects of the small-scale inhomogeneity on the large-scale flow.

The stochastic transport operator includes physically interpretable terms for large-scale representation of flows. The last term on the right-hand side of (
2.4
) is an inhomogeneous diffusion term, representing small-scale mixing. This stochastic diffusion is entirely defined by the variance of the noise, and can be interpreted as a matrix generalization of the Boussinesq eddy viscosity assumption. The third term on the right-hand side represents the transport of the large-scale quantity
q
by the unresolved velocity. Remarkably, the energy associated with this backscattering term is exactly compensated by the stochastic diffusion term
[
46
]
. This equilibrium can be interpreted as an immediate instance of the fluctuation-dissipation theorem.

By interpreting the stochastic diffusion term
-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla q)dt
as the Itō-Stratonovitch correction of the back-scattering Itō noise term
\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla q
in the context of “pure” transports – i.e.
\mathbbm{D}_{t}q=0
– we may define a Stratonovitch transport operator

\mathbbm{D}^{\circ}_{t}q:=d_{t}q+(u-u_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla q\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla q.

Here,
\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla q
denotes a Stratonovitch noise term. Assuming this noise term is well-defined, the two transport operators
are
equivalent in the absence of forcing terms. By this, we mean that
(\mathbbm{D}_{t}q=0)
and
(\mathbbm{D}^{\circ}_{t}q=0)
are equivalent. The importance of distinguishing these transport operators will appear clearer when exploring weak hydrostatic assumptions.

2.1
The LU primitive equations with strong hydrostatic assumption

Let us now derive the LU primitive equations. For modelling purposes, we assume that the flow is isochoric with constant material density. In addition, we suppose that the noise is divergence-free, with a divergence-free corresponding Itō-Stokes drift, i.e.

\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}u=\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}u_{s}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sigma_{t}dW_{t}=0.

(2.5)

From these two assumptions, one can deduce immediately that, for any conservative scalar quantity
q
,

\mathbbm{D}_{t}q=0.

A linear law of state, relating density, salinity and temperature, can be expressed through a Taylor expansion: write the density
\rho
as

\rho=\rho_{0}\Big(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})\Big),

(2.6)

with
\rho_{0}
the reference density of the ocean at a typical temperature
T_{r}
and salinity
S_{r}
. We assume that the thermodynamic parameters
\beta_{T}:=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial T}
and
\beta_{S}:=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial T}
are constant.

Following the derivation of
[
19
]
, we derive the following stochastic equations of motion by applying the SRTT to the conservation of momentum principle in rotating frame,

\mathbbm{D}_{t}u+fk\times(u\>dt+\sigma dW_{t})=-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(u\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}),

(2.7)

where
A^{v}
is a diffusion operator defined below – see equation (
2.10
). Notice that we have introduced, in addition to the classical pressure term
p\>dt
, a martingale noise pressure term
dp_{t}^{\sigma}
arising from the stochastic modelling. Importantly, this martingale pressure term must be interpreted in the Itō formalism, as follows

dp_{t}^{\sigma}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\pi_{k}\>d\beta_{k},

(2.8)

where
(\pi_{k})_{k}
are
\mathbbm{R}
-valued functions, that depend implicitly on the
semimartingale

u
through the divergence-free condition. Upon applying the SRTT to the conservation of energy and saline mass, we also obtain evolution equations on the temperature and salinity,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}T,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbbm{D}_{t}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}S.

(2.9)

In equations (
2.7
) and (
2.9
), we have employed the anisotropic diffusion operators
A^{v}
,
A^{T}
and
A^{S}
. For
i\in\{v,T,S\}
, and given viscosities
\mu_{i},\nu_{i}
specified
a priori
, we define

\displaystyle A^{\scriptscriptstyle i}

\displaystyle=-\mu_{i}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})-\nu_{i}\partial_{zz}.

(2.10)

Denote by
u^{*}=u-u_{s}
, and notice that by assumption
u^{*}
,
u
and
u_{s}
are divergence-free. Therefore, if we denote by
v^{*}
,
v
and
v_{s}
their respective horizontal components, we can express the corresponding vertical components using the integro-differential operator

w(v)=\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v,

(2.11)

under the hypothesis that
w(v)=0
when
z=0
. In particular, this boundary condition will be used to define the function spaces introduced in subsection
3.1
. The horizontal gradient operator is denoted by
\nabla_{H}=(\partial_{x}\>\partial_{y})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
, and we define horizontal Laplace operator as
\Delta_{H}(\boldsymbol{\cdot})=\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\nabla_{H}(\boldsymbol{\cdot}))
. Additionally, let
\sigma^{H}dW_{t}
and
\sigma^{z}dW_{t}
denote the horizontal and vertical components of
\sigma dW_{t}
, respectively. Thus, the horizontal and vertical momentum equations write

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}w=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt,

(2.13)

where
\Gamma((a\hskip 9.24994ptb)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T})=f(-b\hskip 9.24994pta)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
stands for the horizontal projection of the Coriolis term.

As explored in a recent paper
[
1
]
, a simplified system can be obtained considering an assumption similar to the classical deterministic hypothesis. Specifically, assuming that the vertical acceleration is negligible compared to gravitational one
g
, we have

\mathbbm{D}_{t}w+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\ll g.

Under this hypothesis, the vertical momentum equation boils down to

\partial_{z}p+\rho g=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=0.

(2.14)

We refer to this assumption as the
strong hydrostatic hypothesis
. The validity of the hydrostatic balance corresponds to a regime of small ratio
\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{Ri}
, where
\epsilon^{2}=h^{2}/L^{2}
is the squared aspect ratio, with
h
and
L
denoting the vertical and horizontal length scales, respectively. The Richardson number is defined as
Ri=N^{2}/(\partial_{z}v)^{2}
, where
N^{2}=-\frac{g}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}\rho
is the stratification factor given by the Brunt-Väsäilä frequency, and
(\partial_{z}v)^{2}
stands for the squared vertical shear of the horizontal velocity
[
40
]
. In the stochastic setting, the strong hydrostatic balance holds if the noise does not disrupt this regime (see also Remark
3.2
).

Gathering all the points described previously and assuming that the strong hydrostatic hypothesis holds, we eventually obtain the following problem

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}Tdt,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbbm{D}_{t}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}Sdt,

\displaystyle\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v+\partial_{z}w=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}p+\rho g=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=0,

\displaystyle\rho=\rho_{0}(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})).

Interpreting the stochastic transport operator as a material derivative, this system enjoys a similar structure as in the deterministic primitive equations system. The model studied in
[
1
]
essentially corresponds to the one above, with the stochastic diffusion term
\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla(\boldsymbol{\cdot}))
replaced by
\nu_{\sigma}\Delta(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
, where
\nu_{\sigma}>0
is a constant. The authors demonstrated well-posedness results, assuming smooth enough initial conditions, with periodic horizontal boundary conditions and rigid-lid type vertical boundary conditions. This corresponds to a
water world
configuration, also referred to as an
aqua planet
. The main results included local-in-time well-posedness of the model, and global-in-time well-posedness when the horizontal component of the noise is barotropic, i.e. independent of the vertical coordinate
z
.

With this in mind, we aim to prove similar well-posedness results for more general models, where we make a different assumption about the vertical momentum equation, retaining essentially more stochastic terms. This assumption is further referred to as the
weak hydrostatic hypothesis
. These relaxed hydrostatic equilibria correspond to dynamical regimes at the limit of validity of the deterministic hydrostatic assumption, such as high-resolution, non-hydrostatic physical phenomena, including wind- or buoyancy-driven turbulence and deep oceanic convection, where strong enough noise disrupts the strong hydrostatic regime.

2.2
Transitioning from strong to weak hydrostatic hypothesis

To derive our new models, we aim to relax the hydrostatic assumption (
2.14
) and derive a weaker form. For this purpose, we begin with physical remarks on the scaling of the quantities involved in the Navier-Stokes vertical momentum equation.

2.2.1
Scaling the vertical momentum equation

Remind that the vertical velocity
w
fulfils the following,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}w=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt.

(2.15)

Writing the previous equation (
2.15
) in Stratonovitch form yields

\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{\circ}w-\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla\partial_{z}\pi_{k}\>dt-\frac{1}{2Re}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla A^{v}\phi_{k}^{z}\>dt\\
=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt,

(2.16)

where
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla\partial_{z}\pi_{k}\>dt
and
\frac{1}{2Re}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla A^{v}\phi_{k}^{z}\>dt
are additional Itō-Stratonovitch correction terms associated to covariation between the noise term and the martingale pressure and molecular dissipation, respectively. Remind that
(\phi_{k})
stands for the eigenfunctions of the noise operator
\sigma
, and that the functions
(\pi_{k})_{k}
define the martingale pressure term

dp_{t}^{\sigma}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\pi_{k}d\beta_{t}^{k}.

Define
U,W
the typical horizontal and vertical large-scale velocities respectively. By this we mean that the horizontal and vertical velocities
(v,w)
can be expressed as
(v,w)=(U\tilde{v},W\tilde{w})
, where
\tilde{v}
and
\tilde{w}
have the order of the unit. In addition, we define
\tau
, the typical large-scale motion timescale, and
L,H
the typical (horizontal and vertical) length scales for the dynamics of the ocean. Consequently,

U=\frac{L}{\tau}\text{ and }W=\frac{H}{\tau}.

Moreover, we define
L_{\sigma},H_{\sigma}
the unresolved characteristic lengths of the small-scale dynamics. Here,
L_{\sigma}
and
H_{\sigma}
correspond to the mixing length of the unresolved small-scale dynamics. They are not directly related to the size of the large-scale length scales nor to the grid resolution. Define also
\Upsilon_{H},\Upsilon_{z}
the typical scales of the variance tensor components
a_{H}
and
a_{z}
, respectively – of which physical units are
m^{2}/s
, that is the unit of a kinematic viscosity. Hence, since the unresolved small scale is modelled by the noise term
\sigma dW_{t}
, we deduce the following scaled lengths and velocities,

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}=(\Upsilon_{H}\,\tau)^{1/2}\text{ and }H_{\sigma}=(\Upsilon_{z}\,\tau)^{1/2}.

(2.17)

Denote also
U_{S}
and
W_{S}
the Itō-Stokes drift counterparts of
U
and
W
. As the Itō-Stokes drift
u_{s}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}a)
is associated to a bounded variation dynamics, its characteristic time is
\tau
, and

\displaystyle U_{S}=\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{L}=\frac{L_{\sigma}^{2}}{L^{2}}U,\hskip 9.24994ptW_{S}=\frac{\Upsilon_{z}}{H}=\frac{H_{\sigma}^{2}}{H^{2}}W.

(2.18)

Thus we can define
L_{S}:=U_{S}\,\tau
and
H_{S}:=W_{S}\,\tau
. Moreover, we define
\epsilon=\frac{H}{L}
and
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\frac{H_{\sigma}}{L_{\sigma}}
the large scale and small scale aspect ratios, respectively. Thus, the large-scale divergence-free conditions read
\frac{U}{L}=\frac{W}{H}
and
\frac{U_{S}}{L}=\frac{W_{S}}{H}
, that is

\frac{W}{U}=\frac{W_{S}}{U_{S}}=\epsilon.

(2.19)

Using the definition of the Itō-Stokes drift scaling (
2.18
), we also infer
\frac{W_{S}}{U_{S}}=\epsilon_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{-1}
, that is
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\epsilon
. In particular, we may define

r_{\sigma}:=\frac{H_{\sigma}}{H}=\frac{L_{\sigma}}{L}=\frac{H_{S}}{H}=\frac{L_{S}}{L}=\frac{(\Upsilon_{H}\,\tau)^{1/2}}{L}.

(2.20)

Notice that a direct scaling on the divergence-free condition of the correlation tensor leads coherently to
\Upsilon_{z}^{1/2}/\Upsilon_{H}^{1/2}=\epsilon
. With these elements, equation (
2.15
) reads

\frac{W}{\tau}d_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{w}+\frac{W}{\tau}(\tilde{u}-r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+\frac{W}{\tau}r_{\sigma}\Big(\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\Big)\tilde{w}-\frac{W}{2\tau}r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})d\tilde{t}\\
-\frac{\mu W}{L^{2}}(\Delta_{\tilde{H}}+\frac{\nu}{\mu\epsilon^{2}}\partial_{\tilde{z}\tilde{z}})(\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})=-\frac{P}{\rho_{0}H}\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}\>d\tilde{t}-\frac{P_{\sigma}}{\rho_{0}H}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}-g\tilde{\rho}d\tilde{t},

(2.21)

where the tilde notations stand for adimensioned variables and operators. Also, we denote
P=\rho_{0}gH
using the classical hydrostatic assumption, and
P_{\sigma}
the scaling of the martingale pressure term. Furthermore, the term
d_{t}w
is expected to vanish in the primitive equations in the limit of small-aspect ratio, with rate
\epsilon^{2}
– see
[
37
]
. Thus, disregarding the terms
d_{t}w
, we infer that

\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=-\rho_{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w+(\mu\Delta_{H}+\nu\partial_{zz})\sigma_{z}dW_{t}+O(\epsilon^{2}).

Consequently,
\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}
and
\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w
share the same order of magnitude, since the viscous term is dominated by the advection term in turbulent flows. Hence, dividing equation (
2.21
) by
\frac{W}{\tau}
yields

d_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{w}+(\tilde{u}-r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\Big(\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\Big)\tilde{w}-\frac{1}{2}r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})d\tilde{t}\\
-\frac{\mu}{LU}(\Delta_{\tilde{H}}+\frac{\nu}{\mu\epsilon^{2}}\partial_{\tilde{z}\tilde{z}})(\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})=-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\frac{gH}{U^{2}}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}\>d\tilde{t}+\tilde{\rho}d\tilde{t})-r_{\sigma}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}.

(2.22)

Introducing the Reynolds and Froude numbers defined as
Re=\frac{LU}{\mu}
and
Fr^{2}=\frac{U^{2}}{gH}
, respectively, and assuming for simplicity that
\nu\sim\mu\epsilon^{2}
(see
[
37
]
), we obtain

d_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{w}+(\tilde{u}-r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\Big(\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\Big)\tilde{w}-\frac{1}{2}r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})d\tilde{t}\\
-\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})=-\frac{Fr^{-2}}{\epsilon^{2}}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}\>d\tilde{t}+\tilde{\rho}d\tilde{t})-r_{\sigma}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}.

(2.23)

Doing the same computation starting from equation (
2.16
) yields

d_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{w}+(\tilde{u}-r_{\sigma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\circ\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}+\frac{1}{2}r_{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{\pi}_{k}\>d\tilde{t}-\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{w}\>d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})\\
+\frac{1}{2Re}r_{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\tilde{\Delta}\tilde{\phi}_{k}^{z}\>dt=-\frac{Fr^{-2}}{\epsilon^{2}}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}\>d\tilde{t}+\tilde{\rho}d\tilde{t})-r_{\sigma}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}.

(2.24)

Hence, multiplying by
\epsilon^{2}
and taking
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
in equation (
2.23
) – Itō form equation – yields,

-r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}-\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})\Bigg]+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\nabla}(\tilde{a}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})+\tilde{u}_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\Bigg]d\tilde{t}\\
=\Big[Fr^{-2}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}\Big]+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(2.25)

Doing the same computation in equation (
2.24
) – Stratonovitch form equation – yields,

-\epsilon^{2}\Big(r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\circ\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\Big)+r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\tilde{u}_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{\pi}_{k}\>d\tilde{t}\\
+\frac{1}{2Re}r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\sum_{k}(\tilde{\phi}_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\tilde{\Delta}\tilde{\phi}_{k}^{z}\>d\tilde{t}=\Big[Fr^{-2}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}\Big]+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(2.26)

Notice that two new correction terms emerge when transitioning to the Stratonovitch formulation. Additionally, the evolution term
d_{t}w
is implicitly included within the asymptotic notation
O(\epsilon^{2})
. The previous computation will allow to derive two distinct expressions of the martingale pressure term
p^{\sigma}
for a small yet non-zero large-scale aspect ratio
\epsilon
. For this purpose, let us discuss the scaling of
r_{\sigma}
. Reminding that
r_{\sigma}=\frac{(\Upsilon_{H}\,\tau)^{1/2}}{L}
, the condition
r_{\sigma}\gg 1
corresponds to the case of a much greater mixing length than the large scale characteristic length, with the implicit assumption that the unresolved small-scale processes correspond to fast phenomena. Another interpretation is possible by introducing a mesoscopic Reynolds number
Re_{\sigma}:=\frac{UL}{\Upsilon_{H}}
. As
r_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{Re_{\sigma}}
, the condition
r_{\sigma}^{2}\gg 1
is equivalent to
Re_{\sigma}\ll 1
, which implies a larger variance tensor scaling than the intrinsic large-scale eddy viscosity
UL
.

In such case, the terms of order
r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}

are not negligible
compared to those of order
\epsilon^{2}
. Consequently, disregarding the terms order
O(\epsilon^{2})
, equations (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) yield two distinct relations, respectively

-r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[(\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})-\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})\Bigg]+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})+\tilde{u}_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\Bigg]d\tilde{t}\\
=\Big[Fr^{-2}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}\Big],

(2.27)

and

-\epsilon^{2}\Bigg[(r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\circ\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w})-\frac{1}{Re}\tilde{\Delta}(\tilde{\sigma}_{z}dW_{\tilde{t}})\Bigg]+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\tilde{u}_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}(\tilde{\phi}_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{\pi}_{k}\>d\tilde{t}\\
+\frac{1}{2Re}r_{\sigma}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\sum_{k}(\tilde{\phi}_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\tilde{\nabla})\tilde{\Delta}\tilde{\phi}_{k}^{z}\>d\tilde{t}=\Big[Fr^{-2}(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})d\tilde{t}+r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\partial_{\tilde{z}}d\tilde{p}_{\tilde{t}}^{\sigma}\Big].

(2.28)

Due to the neglect of the evolution term
d_{t}w
, these two relations are no longer equivalent. On the one hand, equation (
2.27
) provides explicit expression for both the bounded variation and martingale components of the pressure term. On the other hand, equation (
2.28
) involves a Stratonovitch transport noise
\Big(r_{\sigma}\tilde{\sigma}dW_{\tilde{t}}\circ\tilde{\nabla}\tilde{w}\Big)
, which must be interpreted as a function of
v
. This is because
w=w(v)=\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v\>dz^{\prime}
as imposed by the divergence-free condition. Consequently, the two formulations differ due to the Itō-Stratonovitch correction terms, which are anticipated to be more intricate in the Stratonovitch formulation (
2.28
).

Furthermore, observe that the deterministic hydrostatic hypothesis,
\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}=0
, is, in fact, a
zeroth-order
approximation in
\epsilon
whenever
r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}=o(1)
. This becomes evident as equations (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) independently yield

(\partial_{\tilde{z}}\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})d\tilde{t}=O(\epsilon^{2},r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}).

(2.29)

In addition, the assumption
\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=0
can be justified in the limit of infinitely small aspect ratios by the following argument. As mentioned earlier, by retaining only the martingale terms in either equation (
2.27
) or (
2.28
), we infer

\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}\sim-\rho_{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w,

that is the back-scattering advection term of
w
and the vertical pressure gradient have the same order of magnitude. Thus, we deduce that

dp_{t}^{\sigma}(x,y,z)\sim dp_{t}^{\sigma,S}(x,y)+\rho_{0}\int_{z}^{0}(\sigma dW_{t}(x,y,z)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)w(x,y,z)\>dz^{\prime},

where
p^{\sigma,S}
is a small-scale surface pressure, which is independent of the vertical coordinate
z
. Consequently, the pressure scaling can be expressed as:

P_{\sigma}\sim P_{\sigma}^{S}+\rho_{0}r_{\sigma}H\frac{W}{\tau}=P_{\sigma}^{S}+\rho_{0}r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}U^{2}=P_{\sigma}^{S}+O(r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}),

(2.30)

and hence,

\nabla_{\tilde{H}}P_{\sigma}\sim\frac{P_{\sigma}^{S}}{L}+\rho_{0}r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}\frac{U^{2}}{L}=\frac{P_{\sigma}^{S}}{L}+O(r_{\sigma}\epsilon^{2}),

(2.31)

which implies that the contribution of the vertical martingale pressure gradient term is negligible compared to its horizontal gradient counterpart. In the limit of infinitely small aspect ratios
\epsilon=0
, the horizontal pressure gradient affecting the horizontal dynamics reduces to
\nabla_{\tilde{H}}P_{\sigma}\sim\frac{P_{\sigma}^{S}}{L}
, corresponding to the complete neglect of
\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}
.

To summarise, the approach presented in this subsection can be interpreted as a refinement of the aforementioned zeroth-order approximation of the Navier-Stokes vertical momentum equation in the context of stochastic flows, which is valid when
r_{\sigma}\gg 1
, or equivalently
Re_{\sigma}\ll 1
, with the mesoscopic Reynolds number representing the ratio between the intrinsic large-scale eddy viscosity and the variance tensor scaling.

2.2.2
Deriving a weak hydrostatic hypothesis formulation

Regarding the previous remarks, we neglect only the large-scale contribution of the vertical acceleration in the vertical momentum equation (
2.13
). Consequently, the latter becomes

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}

\displaystyle(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt=\mathbbm{D}_{t}w+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})

\displaystyle\approx-u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)dt+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t}).

(2.32)

This can be expressed as two separate equations,

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}p=-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)+u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w,\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=-\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t}).

(2.33)

We call these relations the
Itō weak hydrostatic hypothesis
. Consequently, the
finite variation
pressure gradients are expressed as,

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p=-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g\>dz^{\prime}-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)\>dz^{\prime}-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p^{s}.

(2.34)

Similarly, and the
martingale
pressure gradients read

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\>dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp^{\sigma,s}_{t}.

(2.35)

Here,
p^{weak}
and
p^{\sigma,weak}
denote, respectively, the finite variation and martingale pressures associated to the relaxation of the hydrostatic hypothesis. Ultimately, the problem under weak hydrostatic balance
(\mathcal{P})
is formulated as:

(\mathcal{P})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),\\
\mathbbm{D}_{t}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}Tdt,\\
\mathbbm{D}_{t}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}Sdt,\\
\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v+\partial_{z}w=0,\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p=\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)-u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\Big]dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p^{s},\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp^{\sigma,s}_{t},\\
\rho=\rho_{0}\Big(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})\Big).\end{array}\right.

(2.36)

Let us emphasise that the model above relies on an important modelling choice. The stochastic integrals in the transport operator (
2.4
) are expressed in Itō form. One could use the Stratonovitch formalism instead – i.e. the operator
\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{\circ}
– leading to the following equation on
w
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}^{\circ}_{t}w=d_{t}w+(u-u_{s})\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla w=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt.

(2.37)

In this case, using the previous scaling argument, the
semimartingale
pressure gradient fulfils

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g\>dz^{\prime}\>dt=-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}\>dt\\
+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\>dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p^{s}dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma,s}).

(2.38)

We refer to this relation as the
Stratonovitch weak hydrostatic hypothesis
. Thus, we define another general problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\circ})
, which reads

(\mathcal{P}^{\circ})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{\circ}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),\\
\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{\circ}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}Tdt,\\
\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{\circ}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}Sdt,\\
\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v+\partial_{z}w=0,\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})=-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g\>dz^{\prime}\>dt-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}\>dt\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p^{s}dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma,s})+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\>dz^{\prime}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\partial_{z}\pi_{k}\>dz^{\prime}\>dt-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\Delta\phi_{k}^{z}\>dz^{\prime}\>dt,\\
\rho=\rho_{0}\Big(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})\Big).\end{array}\right.

(2.39)

In particular, the Stratonovitch transport noise terms appearing in the horizontal velocity dynamics are the following, since the other noise terms are additive,

\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla v+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\circ\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}=\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla v+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}\\
-\frac{1}{2}\Big[\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}a\nabla v+\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\check{a}[\nabla w]+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{a}[\nabla v]dz^{\prime}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{\hat{a}}[\nabla w]\>dz^{\prime}+\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}\Big]dt,

(2.40)

where, with double index summation convention,

\displaystyle\text{for $f$ an $\mathbbm{R}^{3}$-valued vector field},\hskip 9.24994pt\check{a}f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\phi_{k}\otimes\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\phi_{k})dz^{\prime})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},(\in\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 3})

(2.41)

\displaystyle\text{for $M$ an $\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 2}$-valued matrix field},\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{a}M=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(M^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\phi_{k})\>dz^{\prime},(\in\mathbbm{R}^{3})

(2.42)

\displaystyle\text{for $f$ an $\mathbbm{R}^{3}$-valued vector field},\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\hat{a}}f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\Delta_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big(\int_{z^{\prime}}^{0}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\phi_{k})dz^{\prime\prime}\Big)dz^{\prime},(\in\mathbbm{R}^{3})

(2.43)

and

\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{H}(\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}+A^{v}\phi_{k}^{H})+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H})+A^{v}\phi_{k}^{z})dz^{\prime}.

(2.44)

Consequently, different Itō-Stratonovitch correction terms arise from this approach, indicating that the two problems differ significantly. The former may result in physical energy imbalance, even though its derivation is “more direct” since it originates from the 3D LU Navier-Stokes equations. In contrast, the latter involves more complex terms and cannot be interpreted as a straightforward simplification of the 3D LU Navier-Stokes equations. Nevertheless, introducing the transport noise with its "true" Itō-Stratonovich correction ensures
a priori
an energy balance. Both models are analyzed in the following, with a particular focus on
(\mathcal{P})
rather than
(\mathcal{P}^{\circ})
.

In both cases, a significant difficulty arises when addressing such problems: the transport noise
\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w
and the Itō-Stokes drift advection
u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w
in the stochastic pressure term lead to the following contributions in the horizontal velocity dynamics,

\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime},\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}\>dt.

Establishing a suitable energy estimate for global pathwise existence and uniqueness with such terms remains a challenge, primarily because they involve three derivatives of the horizontal velocity
v
, given that
w(v)=\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v
.
In the following, we propose two techniques to regularize problem
(\mathcal{P})
, and one technique to regularize problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\circ})
.

2.2.3
Weak low-pass filtered hydrostatic hypothesis

We study the problem
(\mathcal{P})
first. To enforce greater regularity for the vertical transport noise, we define a regularizing convolution kernel
K
, and replace the Itō weak hydrostatic hypothesis (
2.33
) by

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}p=-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla w)+K*[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]\>dt,\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=-K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t}),

(2.45)

with

a^{K}f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\mathcal{C}_{K}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{K}(\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}f),

(2.46)

where
\mathcal{C}_{K}
is the operator
f\longmapsto K*f
. Notice that the regularising kernel only affects the Itō-Stokes drift advection, the vertical transport noise and the associated diffusion – and not possible vertical additive noises. Moreover, the stochastic diffusion operator
\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla(\boldsymbol{\cdot}))
is chosen to be the covariation correction term associated to
K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(\boldsymbol{\cdot}))]
. We refer to this assumption as the
(Itō) weak (low-pass) filtered hydrostatic hypothesis
. This strategy involves filtering the transport noise of the vertical component and neglecting the vertical acceleration of the resolved vertical velocity. The noise terms, together with the stochastic diffusion term, represent deviations from a strong hydrostatic equilibrium. Convolving the vertical transport noise with
K
effectively removes its highest frequencies. This new hypothesis should be seen as a relaxation of the strong hydrostatic balance, allowing for the consideration of more general stochastic pressures and extending the range of dynamical regimes compared to the strong hydrostatic case. Furthermore, it is worth noticing that applying a filtering kernel is common practice in defining numerical models for the primitive equations. This technique is also frequently employed to establish the well-posedness of specific (mesoscale) subgrid models, such as the Gent-McWilliams model
[
34
]
.

We can compare this approach with that of
[
2
]
, where the authors introduced a temperature noise affecting the pressure equation. In their model, the pressure noise is of
thermodynamic
origin. By contrast, our model considers transport noise in the vertical velocity component, arising from a
mechanical
origin. This approach explicitly retains additional terms dependent on the vertical velocity
w
and captures the influence of unresolved small-scale velocity (e.g., turbulence or submesoscale components) on the vertical large-scale velocity. As a result, the problem formulation is closer to a “true” three-dimensional problem, which formally justifies the use of additional regularization via a filtering kernel. Thus, assuming that this weak hydrostatic hypothesis holds instead of the strong one, the following problem is derived,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),

(2.47)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}Tdt,

(2.48)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}Sdt,

(2.49)

\displaystyle\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v+\partial_{z}w=0,

(2.50)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}p+\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla w)-K*[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]=0,

(2.51)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}+K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})=0,

(2.52)

\displaystyle\rho=\rho_{0}\Big(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})\Big).

(2.53)

The weak filtered hydrostatic hypothesis impacts the horizontal momentum equation through the horizontal pressure gradients, as follows,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g\>dz^{\prime}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla w)\>dz^{\prime}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}K*[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]\>dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p^{s},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w]-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\>dz^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp^{\sigma,s}_{t}.

While new additive noise terms do not pose any issue for a sufficiently regular
\sigma dW_{t}
, regularizing the term
\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}
in the expression of
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp^{\sigma}_{t}
is essential for our theoretical analysis. This term represents the horizontal influence of the vertical transport noise
\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w
. Applying a smoothing filter as a regularization method naturally increases its spatial scale, resulting in the vertical transport noise having a spatial scale larger than the resolution scale. Similarly, the term
\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)\>dz^{\prime}
corresponds to the horizontal influence of the covariation correction arising from the LU Navier-Stokes equations, and has been modified accordingly. The well-posedness of this model, under appropriate regularity and structural conditions, constitutes our main result.

Additionally, we introduce two alternative methods to regularize the vertical dynamics, this time incorporating (hyper)viscosity terms. Such approaches are also commonly employed in numerical implementations of primitive equations. These viscosity-based regularized models are detailed below.

2.2.4
Weak hydrostatic hypothesis with an additional eddy-(hyper)viscosity

Another approach to regularizing the vertical dynamics of
(\mathcal{P})
involves identifying the large-scale contributions of the transport and the molecular diffusion terms associated to
w
in (
2.13
) to a (hyper)diffusion term
\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w
, with
\alpha>0
and
\gamma_{r}\geq 1
. This leads to the following identity,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}w+

\displaystyle A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt+\sigma^{z}dW_{t})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{d_{t}w+u\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dt+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(w\>dt)}_{\approx-\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w\>dt}-u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla wdt+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)dt+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})

\displaystyle\approx-u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)dt+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma dW_{t})+\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w\>dt.

Using (
2.13
) a new relation between the vertical velocity and the pressure is derived,

-u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dt+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)dt+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})+\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w\>dt=-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}gdt,

which implies

\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}p=-\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)+u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w,\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=-\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t}).

(2.54)

We refer to this assumption as the
(Itō) weak eddy-(hyper)viscosity hydrostatic hypothesis
. The introduction of a hyper-viscosity term is, again, common practice for improving the regularity and stability of ocean numerical models. In this paper, we establish the existence of martingale solutions for such systems when
\gamma>2
.

However, it is important to remark that
\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla w)\>dz^{\prime}
is
not
the covariation correction of the horizontal momentum noise term
\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w\>dz^{\prime}
, as mentioned in Subsection
2.2
. For this reason, we investigate how this approach applies to the problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\circ})
.

2.2.5
Weak hydrostatic hypothesis with energy balanced perturbation

Doing the same computation as before in the Stratonovitch formalism yields

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\check{a}[\nabla w]\>dt+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}dt,

(2.55)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}T=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}Tdt,

(2.56)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{D}_{t}S=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}Sdt,

(2.57)

\displaystyle\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}v+\partial_{z}w=0,

(2.58)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}p+\frac{\rho}{\rho_{0}}g=u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{a}[\nabla v]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{\hat{a}}[\nabla w]-\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla\partial_{z}\pi_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\Delta\phi_{k}^{z},

(2.59)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w+A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})=0,

(2.60)

\displaystyle\rho=\rho_{0}\Big(1+\beta_{T}(T-T_{r})+\beta_{S}(S-S_{r})\Big),

(2.61)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}
has been defined in equation (
2.44
). We refer to the equations (
2.59
) and (
2.60
) as the
weak eddy-viscosity energy-balanced hydrostatic hypothesis
. Since we used the “true” energy covariation correction terms, this model is
a priori
balanced in terms of
L^{2}
-energy. However, we keep an extra diffusion term
\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w
on the vertical velocity
w
, to be used to compensate for residual energy terms arising from the use of Itō’s lemma. Our main result, that is Theorem
3
, is only proven when
\gamma_{r}>1
. However, establishing the existence of martingale solutions in the (Newtonian) diffusive case
\gamma_{r}=1
remains a challenge without additional regularisation.

2.2.6
Initial and boundary conditions

In addition, we introduce the initial and boundary conditions we use in our study. Decompose the boundary as
\partial\mathcal{S}=\Gamma_{u}\cup\Gamma_{b}\cup\Gamma_{l}
– respectively the upper, bottom and lateral boundaries – and equip this problem with the following free-slip rigid-lid boundary conditions
[
8
,
17
]
,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}v=0,

\displaystyle w=0,

\displaystyle\nu_{T}\partial_{z}T+\alpha_{T}T=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}S=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma_{u},

(2.62)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}v=0,

\displaystyle w=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}T=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}S=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma_{b},

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{n}_{H}}v\times\vec{n}_{H}=0,

\displaystyle v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}_{H}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{n}_{H}}T=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{n}_{H}}S=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma_{l}.

Moreover, we consider an initial condition
U(t=0)=U_{0}=(v_{0},T_{0},S_{0})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
and an Itō-Stokes drift
U_{s}=(v_{s},0,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
which fulfil (
2.62
).

3
Abstract framework and main results

3.1
Function spaces

3.1.1
Sobolev spaces and noise regularity

In this subsection, we define the Sobolev spaces to be used further. Remind that the spatial domain is denoted by
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{H}\times[-h,0]\subset\mathbbm{R}^{3}
. We define the following function spaces on
\Omega_{S}\in\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}_{H}\}
, for any real numbers
1\leq q<\infty
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and any integers
d\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
,

\displaystyle L^{q}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})=\bigg\{u:\Omega_{S}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{d}\bigg|\int_{\Omega_{S}}\|u\|^{q}<+\infty\bigg\},L^{\infty}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})=\bigg\{u:\Omega_{S}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{d}\bigg|\esssup_{\Omega_{S}}\|u\|<+\infty\bigg\},

\displaystyle W^{m,p}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})=\bigg\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})\bigg|\forall\alpha\text{ such that }|\alpha|\leq m,D^{\alpha}u\in L^{p}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})\bigg\}.

We write
W^{m,2}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})=H^{m}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
for any non negative integer
m
. Additionally, we define the spaces
H^{s}(\Omega_{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
by interpolation, for any positive real number
s
. Furthermore, for any Banach space
\mathcal{B}
, and for
I
an interval of
\mathbbm{R}_{+}
, we denote by
C(I,\mathcal{B})
the set of continuous functions from
I
to
\mathcal{B}
, and the function spaces

\displaystyle L^{p}(I,\mathcal{B})=\Big\{f:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\>\Big|\>\int_{I}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}<\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(I,\mathcal{B})=\Big\{f\in L^{p}(I,\mathcal{B})\>\Big|\>\int_{I}\int_{I}\frac{\|f(t)-f(t^{\prime})\|^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}{|t-t^{\prime}|^{1+sp}}dtdt^{\prime}<\infty\Big\},

with

\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{L^{p}(I,\mathcal{B})}=\Big(\int_{I}\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}dt\Big)^{1/p}\!\!\!\!\!\!,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{W^{s,p}(I,\mathcal{B})}=\Big(\int_{I}\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}dt+\int_{I}\int_{I}\frac{\|f(t)-f(t^{\prime})\|^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}{|t-t^{\prime}|^{1+sp}}dtdt^{\prime}\Big)^{1/p}\!\!\!\!\!\!,

their respectively associated norms – see
[
22
]
.
For any interval
I\subset\mathbbm{R}_{+}
, we also define
W^{s,p}_{loc}(I,\mathcal{B}):=\Big\{f:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\>\Big|\>\forall T>0,f\in W^{s,p}([0,T],\mathcal{B})\Big\}
.
In addition, for any Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we define
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, and
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})}
its associated norm.

In addition, we decompose
\phi_{k}
as
\phi_{k}=(\phi_{k}^{H}\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{k}^{z})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
, where
\phi_{k}^{H}
is a 2D vector and
\phi_{k}^{z}
a scalar. Then, we assume that the noise and its gradient cancel on the boundary – that is
\phi_{k}=\nabla\phi_{k}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Notice that equation (
3.1
) below allows to give a meaning to the value of
\phi_{k}
and
\nabla\phi_{k}
on the boundary. As such,
u_{s}
and
a
cancel on the boundary, and so do the gradients of
a
. Consequently, the transport noise and diffusion terms and their gradients cancel on the boundary, which will be used in our integration by parts arguments. Finally, the noise is assumed to be regular enough in the following sense,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}^{2}<\infty,

(3.1)

\displaystyle u_{s}\in L^{\infty}\Big([0,T],H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})\Big),

\displaystyle d_{t}u_{s}\in L^{\infty}\Big([0,T],H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})\Big),

\displaystyle a\nabla u_{s}\in L^{\infty}\Big([0,T],H^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})\Big),

and

a\in H^{1}\Big([0,T],H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})\Big).

These regularity assumptions are not limiting in practice since most models consider spatially smooth noises for ocean models
[
56
]
, as they are the physically observed ones.

3.1.2
Rigid-lid boundary conditions spaces

Let us define the function spaces associated to the rigid-lid boundary conditions (
2.62
). We define first the following inner products

\displaystyle(v,v^{\sharp})_{H_{1}}=(v,v^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})},\hskip 9.24994pt(v,v^{\sharp})_{V_{1}}=(\nabla v,\nabla v^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle(T,T^{\sharp})_{H_{2}}=(T,T^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})},\hskip 9.24994pt(T,T^{\sharp})_{V_{2}}=(\nabla T,\nabla T^{\sharp})_{L^{2}}+\frac{\alpha_{T}}{\nu_{T}}(T,T^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{u},\mathbbm{R})},

\displaystyle(S,S^{\sharp})_{H_{3}}=(S,S^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})},\hskip 9.24994pt(S,S^{\sharp})_{V_{3}}=(\nabla S,\nabla S^{\sharp})_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}.

Then, let

\displaystyle(U,U^{\sharp})_{H}=(v,v^{\sharp})_{H_{1}}+(T,T^{\sharp})_{H_{2}}+(S,S^{\sharp})_{H_{3}}\text{ and }(U,U^{\sharp})_{V}=(v,v^{\sharp})_{V_{1}}+(T,T^{\sharp})_{V_{2}}+(S,S^{\sharp})_{V_{3}},

(3.2)

for all
U,U^{\sharp}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})
. Also we denote by
\|.\|_{H},\|.\|_{H_{i}}
and
\|.\|_{V},\|.\|_{V_{i}}
the associated norms. With a slight abuse of notation, we may write
\|.\|_{H},\|.\|_{V}
in place of
\|.\|_{H_{i}},\|.\|_{V_{i}}
, respectively. Then, denote by
\mathcal{V}_{1}
the space of functions of
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})
, such that for all
v\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
,
\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\int_{-h}^{0}vdz=0
on
\mathcal{S}_{H}
and
v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}=0
on
\Gamma_{l}
. In addition, define
\mathcal{V}_{2}=\mathcal{V}_{3}
the space of functions of
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})
that average to zero over
\mathcal{S}
. Denote by
H_{i}
the closure of
\mathcal{V}_{i}
for the norm
\|.\|_{H_{i}}
, and
V_{i}
its closure by
\|.\|_{V_{i}}
. Eventually, define
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}\times H_{3}
and
V=V_{1}\times V_{2}\times V_{3}
, which are also the closures of
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}\times\mathcal{V}_{3}
by
\|.\|_{H}
and
\|.\|_{V}
, respectively. Often, by abuse of notation, we write
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{H}
instead of
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{V^{\prime}\times V}
. More generally, if
K
is a subspace of
H
and
K^{\prime}
its dual space, we write
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{H}
instead of
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{K^{\prime}\times K}
. Moreover, we define
\mathcal{D}(A)=V\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})
, where the operator
A
is defined below (
3.7
). As such,
A:\mathcal{D}(A)\rightarrow H
is an unbounded operator.

Notice that, for consistency, we distinguished the spaces
H_{2}
and
H_{3}
even though they formally denote the same space. However,
V_{2}
and
V_{3}

are
different spaces since they are not equipped with the same inner products due to different boundary conditions on the temperature and salinity (Robin and Neumann respectively). This distinction allows to interpret
H_{2}
and
V_{2}
as temperature spaces, and
H_{3}
and
V_{3}
as salinity spaces. In addition,
H_{1}
and
V_{1}
are interpreted as horizontal velocity spaces (
\mathbbm{R}^{2}
-valued processes). Using this formalism, the vertical velocity
w
is written as a functional of the horizontal velocity
v
through the continuity equation, namely
w(v)=\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{H}\cdot v\>dz^{\prime}
.

Eventually, we define the barotropic and baroclinic projectors
\mathcal{A}_{2}:\mathbbm{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{2}
,
\mathcal{A}:\mathbbm{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{3}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathbbm{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{3}
of the velocity component as follows. For
v\in H
,
h
being the depth of the ocean, let

\mathcal{A}_{2}[v](x,y)=\frac{1}{h}\int_{-h}^{0}v(x,y,z^{\prime})dz^{\prime},\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{A}[v](x,y,z)=\mathcal{A}_{2}[v](x,y),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{R}[v]=v-\mathcal{A}[v].

(3.3)

Remark that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{R}
are orthogonal projectors with respect to the inner product
(\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})_{H}
. To simplify notations, we may use
\bar{v}
in place of
\mathcal{A}_{2}[v]
or
\mathcal{A}[v]
, and
\tilde{v}
in place of
\mathcal{R}[v]
. We give some convenient computation rules on the barotropic and baroclinic modes hereafter.

Remark 3.1
(Useful algebraic rules on barotropic and baroclinic modes).

Let
f,g:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
two scalar functions. Then,

\mathcal{A}(fg)=\bar{f}\bar{g}+\mathcal{A}(\tilde{f}\tilde{g}),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{R}(fg)=\bar{f}\tilde{g}+\tilde{f}\bar{g}+\mathcal{R}(\tilde{f}\tilde{g}).

In particular, if
\tilde{f}=0
i.e.
f=\bar{f}
, then

\mathcal{A}(fg)=\bar{f}\bar{g}=f\bar{g},\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{R}(fg)=\bar{f}\tilde{g}=f\tilde{g},

that is
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{R}
commute with the functional
g\mapsto fg
when
f
is barotropic. Additionally, for all
i\in\{x,y\}
, we have

\mathcal{A}\partial_{i}f=\partial_{i}\mathcal{A}f,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{R}\partial_{i}f=\partial_{i}\mathcal{R}f.

If in addition
f=0
on
\Gamma_{u}\cup\Gamma_{b}
, this is also true for
i=z
.

3.2
Abstract formulation of the problems

In this section, we aim to express, in abstract form, the problem under the weak (low-pass) filtered hydrostatic hypothesis (
2.45
). First, define the 4D vector
U
, representing the state of the system, and the correction
U^{*}
of
U
by the Itō-Stokes drift, as

U=(v,T,S)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},\hskip 9.24994ptU^{*}=(v^{*},T,S)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}=(v-v_{s},T,S)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},

(3.4)

and denote the advection operator by

B(U^{*},\boldsymbol{\cdot})=B(v^{*},\boldsymbol{\cdot}):=(v^{*}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{H})(\boldsymbol{\cdot})+w(v^{*})\partial_{z}(\boldsymbol{\cdot}),

(3.5)

whenever it is defined. Here, we make an abuse of notation by identifying
B(U^{*},\boldsymbol{\cdot})
to
B(v^{*},\boldsymbol{\cdot})
. This does not pose a problem, since the advection only depends on the velocity components of
U^{*}
. Then, we define a Leray type projectors
\mathbf{P}^{v}
and
\mathbf{P}
as follows (see
[
8
]
),

\mathbf{P}^{v}v=\mathbf{P}_{2D}\mathcal{A}v+\mathcal{R}v=\mathbf{P}_{2D}\bar{v}+\tilde{v},\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbf{P}U=(\mathbf{P}^{v}v,T,S)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},

(3.6)

where
\mathbf{P}_{2D}
is the 2D Leray projector, subject to the boundary condition
\bar{v}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{H}
, which is associated to the barotropic component
\bar{v}
. Notice that the baroclinic component
\tilde{v}
is left unchanged by the projector
\mathbf{P}^{v}
, that is
\mathbf{P}
only affects the barotropic component of velocity. In addition,
\mathbf{P}
is the identity over the temperature and salinity components.

Also, for notational convenience we keep the same notations for the composition of the following operators with the Leray projector,

AU=\mathbf{P}(A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}v,\>A^{\scriptscriptstyle T}T,\>A^{\scriptscriptstyle S}S)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},\hskip 9.24994ptB(U^{*},U)=\mathbf{P}(B(v^{*},v),\>B(v^{*},T),\>B(v^{*},S))^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma U=\mathbf{P}(\Gamma v,\>0,\>0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}.

(3.7)

With this definition,
A:\mathcal{D}(A)\rightarrow H
is an unbounded operator with
\mathcal{D}(A)=V\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})
. By abuse of notation again, we will often denote
Av
for
\mathbf{P}^{v}A^{v}v
, which is the velocity component of
AU
. This extends to the temperature and salinity, that is we further denote
AT
and
AS
in place of
A^{T}T
and
A^{S}S
respectively, them being the temperature and salinity components of
AU
.

At this point we may state some useful facts: let
v^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}(A)
, so that

\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\bar{v}^{\sharp}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{v}^{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}=\frac{\partial\bar{v}^{\sharp}}{\partial\vec{n}}\times\vec{n}=0\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{S}_{H}.

(3.8)

Consequently,

1.

Using the boundary conditions (
2.62
) on
\Gamma_{u}\cup\Gamma_{b}
, we remark that the anisotropic Laplace operator
A^{v}
fulfils

\mathcal{A}A^{v}v^{\sharp}=\frac{1}{h}\int_{-h}^{0}\big[\mu_{v}(-\Delta_{H})v^{\sharp}+\nu_{v}(-\partial_{zz})v^{\sharp}\big]dz^{\prime}=\mu_{v}(-\Delta_{H})\bar{v}^{\sharp}=A^{v}\bar{v}^{\sharp}.

Thus,
\mathcal{R}A^{v}v=A^{v}\tilde{v}^{\sharp}
.

2.

Following the remark of
[
10
]
, since
v^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}(A)
, then
\Delta_{H}\bar{v}^{\sharp}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{H})
. Therefore,
\Delta_{H}\bar{v}^{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}\in H^{-1/2}(\partial\mathcal{S}_{H})
– see
[
54
]
for instance. Also, using the boundary conditions on
\bar{v}^{\sharp}
, we deduce that
\Delta_{H}\bar{v}^{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{H}

[
58
]
. Hence,
A^{v}v^{\sharp}=\mathbf{P}^{v}A^{v}v^{\sharp}
. Consequently, the operator
A
is equal to
(A^{v},A^{T},A^{S})
.

Combining the previous remarks, we infer as a result,

\mathcal{A}Av^{\sharp}=A\bar{v}^{\sharp}=A^{v}\bar{v}^{\sharp},\text{ and }\mathcal{R}Av^{\sharp}=A\tilde{v}^{\sharp}=A^{v}\tilde{v}^{\sharp}.

(3.9)

In the following, we will assume that
U_{s}=(v_{s},0,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\in V
.

3.2.1
Low-pass filtered problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})

These remarks being made, we aim to derive an abstract formulation for the (low-pass) filtered problem. From the subsection
2.2.3
, we infer

d_{t}U+\Bigg[AU+B(U^{*},U)+\Gamma U+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{H}p\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla U)\Bigg]dt=-\mathbf{P}(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla U)\\
-(A+\Gamma)(\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(3.10)

Reminding the notations
U_{s}=(v_{s},0,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
and
(v_{s},w_{s})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}=u_{s}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}a)
, we rewrite the previous equation in terms of
U^{*}=U-U_{s}
with a change of variable, to get

\displaystyle d_{t}U^{*}+[AU^{*}+B(U^{*})+\Gamma U^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{H}p\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+F_{\sigma}(U^{*})]dt=G_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(3.11)

where the operators
F_{\sigma}
and
G_{\sigma}
are defined as

\displaystyle F_{\sigma}(U^{*})dt

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\Big[d_{t}U_{s}+[B(U^{*},U_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla U_{s})+AU_{s}+\Gamma U_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla U^{*})]dt\Big],

(3.12)

\displaystyle G_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\Big[-(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)U^{*}-(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)U_{s}-A(\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\Gamma(\sigma^{H}dW_{t})\Big].

(3.13)

Moreover, as
u_{s}
is divergence-free,
w_{s}=w(v_{s})
follows from the definition of operator
w(v)
(
2.11
). In addition, we can derive the following relations for the pressure terms, using equations (
2.51
) and (
2.52
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p=

\displaystyle-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p^{s}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s}))\Big]dz^{\prime},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})=

\displaystyle-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w(v^{*})]+K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w_{s}]+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma,s}.

(3.15)

The quantities
p^{s}\>dt
and
dp_{t}^{\sigma,s}
are respectively the bounded variation and the martingale contributions to the surface pressure. As they are independent on the z-axis, we have for all
v^{\sharp}\in V_{1}
,

\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}^{v}\nabla_{H}p^{s},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{H}=-\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}p^{s},\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}\Big)_{H}=0,

using the boundary conditions on
\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}
, which are
\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}=0
on
\mathcal{S}_{H}
and
\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\vec{n}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vec{n}}\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}\times\vec{n}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{H}
. This shows that
\mathbf{P}^{v}\nabla_{H}p^{s}=0
. Similarly, we get
\mathbf{P}^{v}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma,s}=0
. Therefore, we get the following relation,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}^{v}[\nabla_{H}p]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}^{v}\Bigg[-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle-\mathbf{P}^{v}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s}))\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}^{v}[\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}^{v}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg].

On the one hand, the bounded variation surface pressure
p^{s}
can be interpreted as a Lagrange multiplicator associated with the constraint
\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathcal{A}v=0
. This interpretation aligns with the proof proposed by Cao and Titi in the deterministic case
[
10
]
, which highlights that the barotropic mode follows a 2D Navier-Stokes equation, while the baroclinic mode evolves according to a 3D Burgers equation, up to some coupling terms. Remarkably, the bounded variation surface pressure does not affect the baroclinic dynamics, the barotropic mode being divergence-free using the vertical boundary conditions. On the other hand, the martingale surface pressure
p^{s,\sigma}
arises from the stochastic modelling we proposed, and may be seen as a perturbation of the pressure
p^{s}
. This new term
p^{s,\sigma}
affects
a priori
both the barotropic and the baroclinic dynamics. However, in the strong hydrostatic hypothesis the martingale pressure equation simplifies to
\partial_{z}dp_{t}^{\sigma}=0
, that is
dp_{t}^{\sigma}=dp_{t}^{s,\sigma}
(equation (
2.14
)). Consequently, the martingale pressure term becomes completely barotropic, affecting only the barotropic dynamics. This reduction permits the use of methods similar to those employed in the deterministic case.

We are now in position to define
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
, for any
K\in H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})
, the abstract primitive equations problem with weak (low-pass) filtered hydrostatic hypothesis, as follows,

(\mathcal{P}_{K})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}d_{t}U^{*}+[AU^{*}+B(U^{*})+\Gamma U^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p+F_{\sigma}(U^{*})]dt=G_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma},\\
\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\big[K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})]+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg],\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}K*\big[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})\big]dz^{\prime}\\
&&-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s}))dz^{\prime}-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}}\Bigg].\end{array}\end{array}\right.

(3.16)

This problem is equipped with an initial condition to be precised further, and we make the noise regularity assumptions (
3.1
). For notational convenience, we have written
\nabla_{H}p
and
\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}
in place of
(\nabla_{H}p,\>0,\>0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
and
(\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma},\>0,\>0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
respectively. In addition, we further denote by
(\pi_{k})_{k}
and
(\pi_{k}^{s})_{k}
the bases of
L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{H},\mathbbm{R})
such that

\displaystyle dp_{t}^{\sigma}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\pi_{k}d\beta_{t}^{k},\text{ and }dp_{t}^{\sigma,s}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\pi_{k}^{s}d\beta_{t}^{k},

and we apply the same abuse of notation to the bases
(\phi_{k})_{k}
,
(\pi_{k})_{k}
and
(\pi_{k}^{s})_{k}
.

3.2.2
Approximated low-pass filtered problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})

Furthermore, we propose an approximation of the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
in the limit of “quasi-barotropic” flow, that we denote by
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
. Remind first that

\sigma dW_{t}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}d\beta^{k},

(3.17)

where
(\phi_{k})
is a family of functions in
L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})
. In this subsection, we assume that each horizontal component
\phi_{k}^{H}
is either barotropic or baroclinic – i.e. independent of
z
or averaging to
0
along the
z
-axis, respectively. This is equivalent to considering that
\sigma^{H}dW_{t}
is a sum of two independent noises,
\sigma_{BT}^{H}dW_{t}
and
\sigma_{BC}^{H}dW_{t}
, the former one being barotropic and the latter being baroclinic. Due to the divergence-free and boundary conditions on the noise, we denote by

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{BT}

\displaystyle:=\phi_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{k}^{H}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\text{ when $\phi_{k}^{H}$ is barotropic},0\text{ otherwise,}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{BC}
