\displaystyle:=\phi_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{k}^{H}\\
w(\phi_{k}^{H})\end{pmatrix}\text{ when $\phi_{k}^{H}$ is baroclinic},0\text{ otherwise.}

(3.19)

Denoting by
\beta^{k,BT}
and
\beta^{k,BC}
their respectively associated Brownian motions, we obtain

\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{BT}d\beta^{k,BT},\hskip 9.24994pt\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{BC}d\beta^{k,BC}.

(3.20)

As a consequence, since
\beta^{k,BT}
and
\beta^{p,BC}
are independent for all
k
and
p
, the variance tensor
a
can be split into two terms
a_{BT}
and
a_{BC}
, so that

a=a_{BT}+a_{BC},\hskip 9.24994pta_{BT}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{BT}(\phi_{k}^{BT})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},\hskip 9.24994pta_{BC}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{BC}(\phi_{k}^{BC})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}.

(3.21)

Eventually, we define the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
as follows,

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}d_{t}U^{*}+[AU^{*}+B(U^{*})+\Gamma U^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p+\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U^{*})]dt=\hat{G}_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma},\\
\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})]+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg],\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}K*\Big[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})\Big]dz^{\prime}\\
&&-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a^{K}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s}))dz^{\prime}-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}}\Bigg],\end{array}\end{array}\right.

(3.22)

where the operators
\hat{F}_{\sigma}
and
\hat{G}_{\sigma}
are defined hereafter,

\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U^{*})dt=\mathbf{P}\Bigg[d_{t}U_{s}+[B(U^{*},U_{s})+AU_{s}+\Gamma U_{s}]dt\\
-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla v^{*})+\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla\overline{v}^{*})\\
\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla T)\\
\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla S)\end{pmatrix}dt-\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla v_{s})+\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla\overline{v}_{s})\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}dt\Bigg],

(3.23)

and

\hat{G}_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}=\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-A(\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\Gamma(\sigma^{H}dW_{t})\\
-\begin{pmatrix}(\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)v^{*}+(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\overline{v}^{*}\\
(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)T\\
(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)S\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}(\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)v_{s}+(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\overline{v}_{s}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\Bigg].

(3.24)

Similarly, the problem is supplemented with an initial condition to be precised further, and we make the same noise regularity assumptions (
3.1
).

Remark 3.2
.

1.

Let us precise in what sense
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
is an approximation of
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
. Regarding the horizontal momentum equation, we have that

\mathbbm{D}_{t}v+\Gamma(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})=-A^{\scriptscriptstyle v}(v\>dt+\sigma^{H}dW_{t})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}(p\>dt+dp_{t}^{\sigma}),

(3.25)

where the stochastic transport term
\mathbbm{D}_{t}v
reads

\mathbbm{D}_{t}v=d_{t}v+(u^{*}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v+(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla_{3}v)dt.

(3.26)

Denote
r
the typical ratio between the baroclinic and barotropic modes – i.e.
|\tilde{v}|\sim r|\bar{v}|
– and assume that the unresolved scale enjoys the same ratio – i.e.
\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\sim r\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}
. It is physically expected that
r\ll 1
, since
\tilde{v}
is much smaller than
\bar{v}
. On the one hand, expanding the third term on the RHS of (
3.26
) yields,

(\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v=(\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v+(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\overline{v}+(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\tilde{v}.

Hence, the physical energy associated to this martingale transport term reads

\sum_{k}\|(\phi_{k}^{BT}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|(\phi_{k}^{BC}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\overline{v}+(\phi_{k}^{BC}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\tilde{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
=\sum_{k}\|(\phi_{k}^{BT}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|(\phi_{k}^{BC}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\overline{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+O(r^{3}).

(3.27)

On the other hand, splitting the fourth term on the RHS of (
3.26
) yields,

\displaystyle\overline{\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla_{3}v)}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\overline{\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})}

\textstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla_{3}v)

\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 29.5876pt\hskip-29.5876pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-29.5876pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.87222pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{5013555}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4355123}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 29.5876pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 29.5876pt\hskip-29.5876pt\hbox{$\textstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-29.5876pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.87222pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{5013555}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4355123}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 29.5876pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 23.61905pt\hskip-23.61905pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-23.61905pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.90224pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3470892}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3470892}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4002197}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3476587}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 23.61905pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 19.03998pt\hskip-19.03998pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-19.03998pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.92223pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2797984}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2797984}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3226284}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2802575}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 19.03998pt}}.

Therefore, by taking the inner product with
v
, the energy dissipated by the stochastic diffusion is expressed as

\displaystyle(v,\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(

\displaystyle a\nabla_{3}v))_{L^{2}}=(\bar{v},\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\overline{\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+(\tilde{v},\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})+\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})+\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 29.5876pt\hskip-29.5876pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-29.5876pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.87222pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{5013555}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4355123}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 29.5876pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 29.5876pt\hskip-29.5876pt\hbox{$\textstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-29.5876pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.87222pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4347989}{1997221}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{5013555}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4355123}{1997222}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 29.5876pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 23.61905pt\hskip-23.61905pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-23.61905pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.90224pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3470892}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3470892}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{4002197}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3476587}{1594333}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 23.61905pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 19.03998pt\hskip-19.03998pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\tilde{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v})$}\hskip-19.03998pt\hskip-208.74297pt\raisebox{8.92223pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.30748pt}{\resizebox{22207518}{283992}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2797984}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2797984}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{3226284}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2802575}{1285237}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\AC$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-208.74297pt\hskip 19.03998pt}})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=(\bar{v},\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}))_{L^{2}}+(\bar{v},\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a}_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}))_{L^{2}}+(\tilde{v},\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v}))_{L^{2}}+O(r^{3}).

Retaining only the terms which contribute at order
r^{2}
or more to the energy finally yields

\mathbbm{D}_{t}v\approx d_{t}v+(u^{*}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v+(\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v+(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\overline{v}\\
-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}v)dt-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{BC}\nabla_{3}\bar{v})dt=:\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{approx}v.

(3.28)

Replacing
\mathbbm{D}_{t}
by its approximation
\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{approx}
in equation (
3.25
) and reasoning similarly as for deriving
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
, we find the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
.

2.

Notice that, when
\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}=0
(or equivalently
a_{BC}=0
),
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
and
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
are in fact equivalent. This stands as a relaxation of the assumption proposed in
[
1
,
2
]
. There, the authors prove the global-in-time well-posedness of an interpretation of the primitive equations with a noise such that its horizontal component is independent of
z
– that is to say barotropic. Conversely, our approach yields an approximation of the horizontal momentum equation in the limit of small – yet non-zero – baroclinic noise components, provided they are decorrelated with the barotropic ones.

3.

Moreover, we draw attention to the fact that, if we assume the noise is divergence-free and has a barotropic horizontal component that fulfils the boundary condition (
2.62
), then it is bidimensional in the following sense,

\sigma dW_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma dW_{t}^{H}(x,y)\\
0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sigma dW_{t}^{H}(x,y)=0.

Therefore, only compressible (divergent) tridimensional noises can fulfil the barotropic horizontal noise condition, and our results still hold true in this case – see Remarks
4.4
and
4.6
in subsection
4.2
. However, for such noises the expression of the Itō-Stokes drift becomes
u_{s}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}a-\sum_{k}\phi_{k}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\phi_{k})
– see
[
55
]
. This drift velocity still fulfil the regularity conditions (
3.1
) for a regular enough noise
\sigma dW_{t}
.

4.

The aforementioned barotropic horizontal noise assumption can be related to the validity range of the primitive equations. The (deterministic) primitive equations are physically valid when the squared aspect ratio
\epsilon^{2}:=(h/L)^{2}
is negligible compared to the Richardson number
Ri:=\frac{N^{2}}{(\partial_{z}v)^{2}}

[
40
]
. Here
v
denotes the horizontal velocity,
h
refers to the depth of the ocean,
L
to the horizontal scale (e.g
\sqrt{|\mathcal{S}_{H}|}
), and
N^{2}=-\frac{g}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{z}\rho
. This condition reads

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{Ri}\ll 1,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ or equivalently }\hskip 9.24994pt(\partial_{z}v)^{2}\ll\frac{N^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}.

(3.29)

In particular, the latter holds in the limit of small enough vertical shear of the horizontal component. In such a case, the horizontal component of the velocity is almost independent of z – therefore it is, so to say, “quasi-barotropic”. In the context of stochastic flows, the horizontal noise models a small scale velocity denoted by
\eta^{1/2}v^{\prime}
, where
\eta^{1/2}
is a scaling factor so that
v
and
v^{\prime}
have the same order of magnitude. Thus, the condition (
3.29
) becomes

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}(v+\eta^{1/2}v^{\prime}))^{2}\ll\frac{N^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ which also reads }\hskip 9.24994pt(\partial_{z}v)^{2},\>\eta(\partial_{z}v^{\prime})^{2}\ll\frac{N^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}.

(3.30)

Therefore, the LU stochastic primitive equations are physically valid under the condition (
3.29
), and when the horizontal noise modelling
\eta^{1/2}v^{\prime}
is either small enough (
\eta\rightarrow 0
) or quasi-barotropic (
(\partial_{z}v^{\prime})^{2}\rightarrow 0
). In this setting, a purely barotropic noise is equivalent to
\partial_{z}v^{\prime}=0
. The approximated problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
corresponds to a setting where
\partial_{z}v
and
\partial_{z}v^{\prime}
are both small, yet non-zero.

5.

Furthermore, assuming
\sigma_{BT}dW_{t}
and
\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}
are independent is crucial for the energy splitting argument to be valid in equation (
3.27
). Otherwise, more terms of order
r^{2}
would emerge, for example
((\phi_{k}^{BT}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})v,(\phi_{k}^{BC}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\tilde{v})_{L^{2}}
, the term
\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v}
becoming non-negligible in the expression of
\mathbbm{D}_{t}^{approx}v
. Such assumption removes a direct
martingale
dependence on
\nabla_{3}\tilde{v}
in the barotropic equation, which allows to use similar estimates as in
[
1
,
2
]
. Not doing so would imply keeping the term
\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}\tilde{v}
in the horizontal momentum equation, and estimate
a priori

\tilde{v}
in
H^{2}
, while classically it is estimated in a (much larger)
L^{p}
-space.

3.2.3
Eddy-viscosity problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})

Additionally, for any
\gamma_{r}\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
, we define
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})
, the abstract primitive equations problem with weak eddy-viscosity hydrostatic hypothesis, as follows,

(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}d_{t}U^{*}+[AU^{*}+B(U^{*})+\Gamma U^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p+F_{\sigma}(U^{*})]dt=G_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma},\\
\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg],\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}\Bigg]+\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})dz^{\prime}\Bigg]\\
&+&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s}))-\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg].\end{array}\end{array}\right.

(3.31)

Again, the problem is supplemented with an initial condition to be precised further, and we make the same noise regularity assumptions (
3.1
).

3.2.4
(Eddy-viscosity) energy-balanced problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})

In a similar fashion, for any
\gamma_{r}\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
, we define
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})
, the abstract primitive equations problem with weak eddy-viscosity energy-balanced hydrostatic hypothesis, as follows,

(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}d_{t}U^{*}+[AU^{*}+B(U^{*})+\Gamma U^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p+F_{\sigma}(U^{*})]dt\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt=G_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\check{a}[\nabla w]dt+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}dt,\\
\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}(dp_{t}^{\sigma})&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w(v^{*})+\sigma dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla w_{s}+A(\sigma^{z}dW_{t})\Big]dz^{\prime}\Bigg],\\
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p&=&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}}\Bigg]+\mathbf{P}\Bigg[-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla(w(v^{*})+w_{s})\Big]\Bigg]\\
&+&\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{a}[\nabla v]+\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\hat{\hat{a}}[\nabla w]\Big)dz^{\prime}-\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\alpha(-\Delta)^{\gamma_{r}}w(v^{*})dz^{\prime}\Bigg]\\
&+&\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}\Bigg[\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\sum_{k}\Big(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla\partial_{z}\pi_{k}+(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\Delta\phi_{k}^{z}\Big)dz^{\prime}\Bigg].\end{array}\end{array}\right.

(3.32)

Once again, the initial condition is precised further, and we make the same noise regularity assumptions (
3.1
).

3.3
Main results

We are now in position to state our main results. We remind that, in the definition of martingale solutions, the stochastic basis – that is the filtered probability space and the Wiener process – is unknown
a priori
. Our main result concerns the well-posedness of the filtered problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
and its approximation
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
,

Theorem 1
.

Suppose
K\in H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})
. Then, the following propositions hold,

1.

Equip the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– resp.
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
– with the initial condition
U_{0}=(v_{0},T_{0},S_{0})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\in H
. Then,
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– resp.
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
– admits at least one global-in-time martingale solution
(\mathcal{S}_{0},U)
, the stochastic basis
\mathcal{S}_{0}
not being fixed
a priori
. In addition, for all
T>0
,

U\in L^{2}\Big(\Omega_{0},L^{2}\big([0,T],V\big)\Big)\cap L^{2}\Big(\Omega_{0},L^{\infty}\big([0,T],H\big)\Big),

where
\Omega_{0}
is the sample space of associated to
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

2.

Assume that
U_{0}\in V
and fix a stochastic basis
a priori
. Then, there exists a stopping time
\tau>0
such that
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– resp.
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
– admits a local-in-time pathwise solution
U^{*}
, which fulfils, for all
T>0
and for all stopping time
0<\tau^{\prime}<\tau
,

U_{\tau^{\prime}\wedge\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\in L^{2}\Big(\Omega,L^{2}\big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A)\big)\Big)\cap L^{2}\Big(\Omega,C([0,T],V)\Big),

where
\Omega
denotes the sample space associated to the aforementioned stochastic basis. This solution is unique up to indistinguishability, that is for all solutions
U^{*}
and
\hat{U}^{*}
of
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– resp.
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
– associated to the stopping times
\tau,\hat{\tau}
respectively, the following holds,

\mathbbm{P}\Big(\sup_{[0,T]}\|U_{\tau^{\prime}\wedge\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}-\hat{U}_{\tau^{\prime}\wedge\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}=0;\hskip 9.24994pt\forall T>0\Big)=1,

for all stopping time
0<\tau^{\prime}<\tau\wedge\hat{\tau}
.

Moreover, under further assumption, we may state a global-in-time well-posedness result.

Theorem 2
.

Using the same notations as in Theorem
1
, assume that
U_{0}\in V
. Moreover, choose
K\in H^{15/4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})
. Then, the following propositions hold,

1.

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
admits a global-in-time pathwise solution, which is unique up to indistinguishability, and almost surely belongs to the space

L_{loc}^{2}\Big([0,+\infty),\mathcal{D}(A)\Big)\cap C\Big([0,+\infty),V\Big).

2.

This solution is continuous in the following sense: let
T>0
and define
\Sigma
, a space of noise operators, as follows,

\Sigma=\Bigg\{\sigma\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\Big(\mathcal{W},H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})\Big)\>\Bigg|\>a\in H^{1}\Big([0,T],H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})\Big)\Bigg\}.

Let
U_{0}^{n}\in V
a sequence of initial conditions, and
\sigma^{n}\in\Sigma
a sequence of noise operators, such that

U_{0}^{n}\rightarrow U_{0}\text{ in $V$, and }\sigma^{n}\rightarrow\sigma\text{ in $\Sigma$.}

Denote by
U^{n}
the solution to
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, associated to the initial condition
U_{0}^{n}
and the noise operator
\sigma^{n}
. Similarly, denote by
U
the solution associated to
U_{0}
and
\sigma
. Then,

U^{n}\rightarrow U\text{ in probability, in the space $L^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A))\cap C([0,T],V)$.}

(3.33)

Remark 3.3
.

•

In particular, for a fixed initial data, if we denote by
U^{\sigma}
the solution of
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
associated to the noise data
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then, in probability,

U^{\Upsilon^{1/2}\sigma}\underset{\Upsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}U^{0},\text{ in }L^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A))\cap C([0,T],V).

Here,
U^{0}
denotes the solution to the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
with noise zero – i.e.
\sigma=0
– that is to say the classical deterministic primitive equations.

•

In particular again, assuming that the noise
\sigma dW_{t}
is purely barotropic, Theorem 2 holds for
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
, because in such case
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
and
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
are equivalent. Using the formalism of subsection
3.2.2
, pathwise solutions of the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
are also continuous as
\sigma_{BC}
varies in
\Sigma
. Denote by
\hat{U}^{\sigma_{BT},\Upsilon^{1/2}\sigma_{BC}}
and
U^{\sigma_{BT}}
the unique pathwise solutions of
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
and
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
, with noise operators
\hat{\sigma}=\sigma_{BT}+\Upsilon^{1/2}\sigma_{BC}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{BT}
, respectively. Consequently, the following convergence happens in probability whenever
\Upsilon_{n}\rightarrow 0
,

\hat{U}^{\sigma_{BT},\Upsilon_{n}^{1/2}\sigma_{BC}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}U^{\sigma_{BT}}.

Moreover, the following weaker existence properties hold for the problems
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})
and
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})
– equations (
3.32
) and (
3.31
) respectively,

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that
\alpha>0
and
\gamma_{r}>1
. Then the problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})
, equipped with the initial condition
U_{0}\in H
, admits at least one global-in-time martingale solution, for all
T>0
, in the space

L^{2}\Big(\Omega,L^{2}([0,T],V)\Big)\cap L^{2}\Big(\Omega,L^{\infty}([0,T],H)\Big).

Theorem 4
.

Suppose that
\alpha>0
and
\gamma_{r}>2
. Then the problem
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})
, equipped with the initial condition
U_{0}\in H
, admits at least one global-in-time martingale solution, for all
T>0
, in the space

L^{2}\Big(\Omega,L^{2}([0,T],V)\Big)\cap L^{2}\Big(\Omega,L^{\infty}([0,T],H)\Big).

To summarise, we show a similar well-posedness result as in
[
1
]
, but with rigid-lid boundary conditions instead of the water world ones, and where we consider additional terms stemming from our discussion about the relaxation of the hydrostatic assumption in a stochastic context. We detail the proof of the first point of Theorem
1
in section
4
, and of its second point in section
5
. Theorem
2
is shown in section
6
. For Theorems
3
and
4
, we only explicit the arguments where the proofs change, since, in
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EV})
and
(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{r}}_{EB})
, only the pressure terms differ from problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
. This is sketched in the appendix.

To be more precise, the sketch of our proof for Theorem
1
is similar to the one used in
[
8
,
17
,
19
]
: we consider a Galerkin approximation of the problem, then, using energy estimates and tightness arguments, we show that a subsequence of its solutions converges toward a solution of the initial problem. Our scheme of proof is closer in spirit to
[
8
]
than to
[
1
]
. Moreover, our proofs adapt when the vertical acceleration
\mathbbm{D}_{t}w
is totally neglected, which corresponds to choosing
K=0
and neglecting additive noises in the pressure equations of the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
. In such case, the problem is equivalent to the one with strong hydrostatic assumption.

4
Existence of martingale solutions

In this section, we aim to show the first point of Theorem
1
concerning
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– equation (
3.16
). For
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, the additional terms will be treated

•

in Remarks
4.1
and
4.2
, concerning preliminary results,

•

in Remarks
4.3
and
4.5
, concerning bounds for the energy estimates.

Moreover, we quickly discuss the influence of non divergence-free noises on the energy estimates in Remarks
4.4
and
4.6
. To be precise, considering such noises would affect the expression of the Itō-Stokes drift, which would become

u_{s}=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}\phi_{k})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\phi_{k}\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}).

All other conditions being fulfilled, the following results hold with the noise regularity assumptions (
3.1
). To emphasise the difference between
\nabla_{H}
and
\nabla
, from now on we use the notation
\nabla_{3}
instead of
\nabla
. For similar reasons, we use
\sigma_{3}
and
a_{3}
for
\sigma
and
a
respectively. In addition, for notational convenience, we may write the transport terms
f\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}g
instead of
(f\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})g
for two differentiable vectors
f
and
g
. Moreover, we remind that
K
has been defined as a regularising kernel of regularity
H^{3}
. In particular, the operator
\mathcal{C}_{K}(\boldsymbol{\cdot}):=K*(\boldsymbol{\cdot})
satisfies

\|\mathcal{C}_{K}f\|_{H^{k}}\leq C(1+\|f\|_{L^{2}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall k\in\{0,1,2,3\}.

(4.1)

The same property holds for the operator
\mathcal{C}_{\check{K}}
.

As mentioned earlier, the sketch of our proof is similar to the approach used in
[
8
,
17
,
19
]
: first we consider a well-chosen range of Galerkin approximation problems, each of them admitting a unique solution. Then we show they fulfil a uniform energy estimate, and that their distributions are tight. By application of the Prohorov and Skorohod theorems, and using limit theorems, this implies the existence of a martingale solution. Eventually, we show an additional regularity result on this solution.

4.1
Preliminaries

Denote by
P^{n}
the projection onto
H_{n}=Span(e_{i})_{i\leq n}\subset H
, the space generated by the
n
first eigenvectors
(e_{i})_{i\leq n}
of
A
(see below for their precise definition), and define the following
H_{n}
-valued operators,

\displaystyle B^{n}=P^{n}B,\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{n}=P^{n}\Gamma,\hskip 9.24994ptF_{\sigma}^{n}=P^{n}F_{\sigma},\hskip 9.24994ptG_{\sigma}^{n}=P^{n}G_{\sigma},

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big)^{n}=P^{n}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big),\hskip 9.24994pt\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma}\Big)^{n}=P^{n}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma}\Big).

Then, denote by
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
the projection of the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
onto
H_{n}
, so that

U_{n}^{*}=P^{n}(U_{0}^{*})-\int_{0}^{t}[AU_{n}^{*}+B^{n}(U_{n}^{*})+\Gamma^{n}U_{n}^{*}+\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big)^{n}+F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})]dr\\
+\int_{0}^{t}[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}-\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma}\Big)^{n}].\>\>\>(\mathcal{P}_{n})

(4.2)

Additionally, the problems
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
are equipped with the initial condition
P^{n}U_{0}\in H_{n}
. Applying the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem for stochastic partial differential equations in finite dimension, we find that each problem
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
admits a local-in-time solution
(U_{n}^{*},t_{n})
, where
U_{n}^{*}\in H_{n}
. The objective of this section is to prove continuity results on the non projected operators involved in the abstract problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
, to be used in the next steps of the proof.

Diffusion, advection and Coriolis operators (
A,B
and
\Gamma
)

Begin with the diffusion operator
A
. Remind first that
\mathcal{D}(A)=V\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})
, then for all
U,U^{\sharp}\in V
, we have

\displaystyle(AU,U^{\sharp})_{H}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}D_{\mu}\partial_{x}U\boldsymbol{\cdot}\partial_{x}U^{\sharp}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}D_{\mu}\partial_{y}U\boldsymbol{\cdot}\partial_{y}U^{\sharp}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}D_{\nu}\partial_{z}U\boldsymbol{\cdot}\partial_{z}U^{\sharp},

(4.3)

with
D_{\mu}=(\mu_{v},\mu_{T},\mu_{S})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
,
D_{\nu}=(\nu_{v},\nu_{T},\nu_{S})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
, since
A=(A^{v},A^{T},A^{S})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle 3\min_{i}\{\mu_{i},\nu_{i}\}\|U\|_{V}^{2}\leq|(AU,U)_{H}|\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and}\hskip 9.24994pt|(AU,U^{\sharp})_{H}|\leq 3\max_{i}\{\mu_{i},\nu_{i}\}\|U\|_{V}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V},

(4.4)

so in particular
\|AU\|_{V^{\prime}}\leq C\|U\|_{V}
. Since
V\hookrightarrow H
is a compact embedding using Sobolev theorem, we deduce that the embedding
\mathcal{D}(A)\hookrightarrow H
is also compact. Consequently
A:\mathcal{D}(A)\longrightarrow H
is a closed positive self-adjoint operator with compact resolvent. By spectral theorem, there exists a family
(e_{i})
of eigenvectors of
A
, such that
(e_{i})
is a Hilbert basis of
H
. Also, each eigenvector
e_{i}
is associated to an eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbbm{R}
, the family
(\lambda_{i})
being increasing and unbounded. We further define, for
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha})=\{u\in H|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}^{2\alpha}|(u,e_{k})|^{2}<\infty\},

(4.5)

and remark that
(e_{i})\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha}),\forall\alpha>0
. Now equip this space with the inner product

\displaystyle(u,v)_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha})}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}^{2\alpha}(u,e_{k})(v,e_{k}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall u,v\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha}).

(4.6)

One can show that, if
\alpha>0
, then the norms
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha})}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{H^{2\alpha}}
are equivalent on
\mathcal{D}(A)
since
\mathcal{S}
is smooth. As a consequence,
\mathcal{D}(A^{\alpha})
is a Hilbert space.

To study the operator
B
, we define, for
U,U^{\flat}\in V
and
U^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}(A)
,

\displaystyle b(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})=\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathcal{S}}v^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)v^{\sharp}+v^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}v^{\sharp}\\
\int_{\mathcal{S}}T^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)T^{\sharp}+T^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}T^{\sharp}\\
\int_{\mathcal{S}}S^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)S^{\sharp}+S^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}S^{\sharp}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)U^{\sharp}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}U^{\sharp}.

(4.7)

Using integration by parts and that
(v,w(v))^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
is divergence-free, remark that
b(u,\boldsymbol{\cdot},\boldsymbol{\cdot})
is anti-symmetric, that is

\displaystyle b(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})=-b(U,U^{\flat},U^{\sharp})=\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)U^{\sharp}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}U^{\sharp}.

(4.8)

To show the continuity of
b
, we split it into two terms
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
as follows,

\displaystyle b_{1}:=\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}(v\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)U^{\sharp},\hskip 18.49988ptb_{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{S}}U^{\flat}w(v)\partial_{z}U^{\sharp}.

(4.9)

Regarding
b_{1}
, using Cauchy-Schwarz and Hölder inequality yields,

\displaystyle|b_{1}(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|\leq\int_{\mathcal{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|U_{j}^{\flat}v_{i}||\partial_{i}U_{j}^{\sharp}|

\displaystyle\leq\|U^{\flat}\otimes U\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 4})}\|\nabla U^{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 4})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{\flat}\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\|U\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V},

and by Sobolev theorem
\|U^{\prime}\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\leq C\|U^{\prime}\|_{V},\>\forall U^{\prime}\in V.
Thus

\displaystyle|b_{1}(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|\leq C\|U^{\flat}\|_{V}\|U\|_{V}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V}.

Regarding
b_{2}
,

\displaystyle|b_{2}(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|\leq C\|w(U)\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{z}U^{\sharp}\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\|U^{\flat}\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\leq\|U\|_{V}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}\|U^{\flat}\|_{V},

using similar arguments. Consequently, we reach

\displaystyle|b(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|

\displaystyle\leq\|U\|_{V}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}\|U^{\flat}\|_{V}.

(4.10)

As demonstrated in
[
45
]
(Lemma 3.1), we also have

\displaystyle|b(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{\flat}\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V}^{1/2}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{1/2}\|U\|_{V}^{1/2}\|U\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{1/2}.

(4.11)

Now, let
B(U,U^{\sharp})=\mathbf{P}b(U,U^{\sharp},\boldsymbol{\cdot})
and
B(U)=B(U,U)
, so that the notation is consistent with the one we proposed earlier. The previous results yield

\displaystyle|B(U,U^{\sharp})|_{V^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}\|U\|_{V},\text{ and }|B(U,U^{\sharp})|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-1})}\leq C\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V}\|U\|_{V},

(4.12)

that is the operator
B
is continuous from
V\times V
to
\mathcal{D}(A^{-1})
, and from
V\times\mathcal{D}(A)
to
V^{\prime}
. To extend this operator to
H\times H
, using the Sobolev theorem, we claim that the embedding

H^{\beta}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})\text{ is continuous}

(4.13)

when
\beta>3/2
. So the embedding

\mathcal{D}(A^{\beta/2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})\text{ is continuous as well,}

(4.14)

with the same condition on
\beta
. As a result, if
U,U^{\sharp}\in V
and
U^{\flat}\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\frac{\beta+2}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle|b(U,U^{\sharp},U^{\flat})|=|b(U,U^{\flat},U^{\sharp})|\leq C\|AU^{\flat}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H}\|U\|_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C\|AU^{\flat}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\beta/2})}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H}\|U\|_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{\flat}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\frac{\beta+2}{2}})}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H}\|U\|_{H}.

Hence,
b
can be extended by continuity on
H\times H\times\mathcal{D}(A^{\gamma})\longrightarrow\mathbbm{R}
, that is
B
can be extended on

H\times H\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
, where
\gamma:=\frac{\beta+2}{2}>\frac{5}{4}
, and satisfies

\displaystyle\|B(U,U^{\sharp})\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})}\leq C\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H}\|U\|_{H}.

(4.15)

We deduce that the operator
B
is locally Lipschitz in
H\times H\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
. Additionally, remark that

\displaystyle|(\Gamma U,U^{\sharp})_{H}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{S}}|f(k\times v)\boldsymbol{\cdot}v^{\sharp}|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}\|v\|_{H}\|v^{\sharp}\|_{H}\leq C\|U\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H},\>\>\>\forall U^{\sharp}\in H.

(4.16)

Bounded variation pressure operator (
\nabla_{H}p
)

Let
U\in H
and
U^{\sharp}\in V
. For
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p
,

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p,U^{\sharp}\Big)_{H}=

\displaystyle-\Big(g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle-\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}))+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}))\Big]dz^{\prime},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle-\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w_{s})\Big]dz^{\prime},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}.

(4.17)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\Big|\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{g(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S)dz^{\prime}},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|=\Big|\Big(g(\beta_{T}T+\beta_{S}S),w(v^{\sharp})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|U\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{V},

\displaystyle\Big|\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w_{s})\Big]dz^{\prime},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}},

where the latter inequality holds immediately by the noise regularity assumption (
3.1
), since the left factor in the last inner product depends only on
v^{\sharp}
and stochastic parameters – that are
u_{s}=(v_{s}\hskip 9.24994ptw_{s})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}a
and
a_{3}^{K}
. In addition, for
U^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}(A^{3/2})
,

\displaystyle\Big|\Big(\nabla_{H}

\displaystyle\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}))+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}))\Big]dz^{\prime},v^{\sharp}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\Big(K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}))+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*})),w(v^{\sharp})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\Big(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*}),\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|+\frac{1}{2}\Big|\Big(w(v^{*}),\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v^{\sharp}))\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\Big|\Big(v^{*},\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{3}((\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp}))\otimes u_{s})dz^{\prime}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|}_{\leq C\|U^{*}\|_{H}\|u_{s}\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\|\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp})\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}}+\frac{1}{2}\Big|\Big(v^{*},\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v^{\sharp}))dz^{\prime}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|U^{*}\|_{H}(\|\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp})\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}+\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{3/2})})\leq C\|U^{*}\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{3/2})},

where we used that the embedding
H^{4}(\mathcal{S})\hookrightarrow W^{2,\infty}(\mathcal{S})
is compact by Sobolev theorem. Therefore,

\|\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-3/2})}\leq C(1+\|U\|_{H}).

(4.18)

Consequently
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-3/2})
is continuous and Lipschitz.

Noise related operators (
F_{\sigma},G_{\sigma}
and
\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}
):

Remind that
\phi_{k}(t)
is a collection of eigenvectors of the operator
\sigma_{3}
, so that one can decompose
\sigma_{3}dW_{t}
and
a_{3}
as follows,

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}dW_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}d\beta_{t}^{k},\hskip 9.24994pta_{3}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}.

(4.19)

Also, remind that the noise
\sigma_{3}dW_{t}
is regular in the following sense: for all
T\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}^{2}<\infty,

\displaystyle U_{s}\in L^{\infty}([0,T],H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})),

\displaystyle d_{t}U_{s}\in L^{\infty}([0,T],H^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})),

\displaystyle a_{3}\nabla U_{s}\in L^{\infty}([0,T],H^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{4})).

Therefore, by Sobolev embedding theorem,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,\hskip 9.24994pt\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|a_{3}\|_{H^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}+\|a_{3}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})}<\infty,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|B(\phi_{k},v_{s})|^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,\hskip 9.24994pt\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u_{s}\|_{H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3\times 3})}<\infty.

Then we want to show that
F_{\sigma}
is Lipschitz. Let
U\in H
and
U^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\gamma})
, then

\displaystyle(F_{\sigma}(U)dt,U^{\sharp})_{H}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(d_{t}U_{s}+\mathbf{P}\Big[B(v,U_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}U_{s})+AU_{s}+\Gamma U_{s}\Big]dt-\mathbf{P}\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}U^{*})dt,U^{\sharp}\Bigg)_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|U\|_{H}+1)\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}dt+(B(v,U_{s}),U^{\sharp})_{H}dt

(4.21)

Notice that

\displaystyle|(B(v,U_{s}),U^{\sharp})_{H}|\leq C\|U\|_{H}\|U_{s}\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\gamma})}.

Therefore,

\|F_{\sigma}(U)\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})}\leq C(\|U\|_{H}+1).

(4.22)

So
F_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
is continuous and Lipschitz.

Remark 4.1
.

Considering the approximated problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, we may split the operator
\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U^{*})
as

\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U^{*})=F_{\sigma}^{BT}(U^{*})-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla\overline{v}^{*})\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla\overline{v}_{s})\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(4.23)

where
F_{\sigma}^{BT}
stands for the operator
F_{\sigma}
of the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
with
\sigma=\sigma_{BT}
– i.e.
a=a_{BT}
. By similar arguments, it is immediate that
\hat{F}_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
is continuous and Lipschitz as well.

We show the same for the noise operators
G_{\sigma}
and
p_{t}^{\sigma}
. Let
\Psi\in\mathcal{W}
, so that

\displaystyle(G_{\sigma}(U)\Psi,U^{\sharp})_{H}=-(\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}U+\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}U_{s}+A(\sigma^{H}\Psi)+\Gamma(\sigma^{H}\Psi),U^{\sharp})_{H}.

(4.24)

Thus,

\displaystyle|(G_{\sigma}(U)\Psi,U^{\sharp})_{H}|\leq C[\|\sigma\Psi\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}+\|\sigma\Psi\|_{H}\|U\|_{H}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{\gamma})}],

that is

\|G_{\sigma}(U)\Psi\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})}\leq C(1+\|U\|_{H})\|\sigma\Psi\|_{H}.

(4.25)

Hence,
G_{\sigma}\Psi:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
is Lipschitz, so
G_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))
is Lipschitz as well, since
G_{\sigma}
is linear with respect to
\sigma
.

Remark 4.2
.

Considering the approximated problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
again, we may split
\hat{G}_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}
as

\hat{G}_{\sigma}(U^{*})dW_{t}=G_{\sigma}^{BT}(U^{*})dW_{t}-\begin{pmatrix}(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\overline{v}^{*}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}(\sigma_{BC}dW_{t}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla)\overline{v}_{s}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(4.26)

where
G_{\sigma}^{BT}
stands for the operator
G_{\sigma}
of the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
with
\sigma=\sigma_{BT}
. Then, it is immediate that
\hat{G}_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))
is continuous and Lipschitz.

Moreover, for
U^{\sharp}\in H
,

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}(\Psi),v^{\sharp})=(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v)+\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s}]+A(\sigma^{z}\Psi)\Big]dz^{\prime},v^{\sharp})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=(K*[\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v)+\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s}]+A(\sigma^{z}\Psi),w(v^{\sharp}))_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=-(v,\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\nabla_{3}[(\sigma_{3}\Psi)\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp})])_{L^{2}}+(\sigma_{3}\Psi\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s},\check{K}*w(v^{\sharp}))_{L^{2}}+(A(\sigma^{z}\Psi),w(v^{\sharp}))_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|U^{*}\|_{H}+1)\|\sigma_{3}\Psi\|_{H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}\|U^{\sharp}\|_{H},

(4.27)

where we used again the boundary conditions on
\mathcal{A}v^{\sharp}
to cancel the martingale surface pressure term. Therefore,

\|\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}(\Psi)\|_{H}\leq C(\|U^{*}\|_{H}+1)\|\sigma_{3}\Psi\|_{H^{4}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})},

(4.28)

so that
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}\Psi:H\rightarrow H
is continuous and Lipschitz. Thus so is
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)
, as
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}
is linear with respect to
\sigma
.

4.2
Energy estimates in
L^{\infty}([0,T],H)\cap L^{2}([0,T],V)
for all
T>0

In the rest of the paper, we assume that the constant
\gamma
used for defining the extension of the operator
B
in equation (
4.15
) fulfils
\gamma=2
. Moreover, for notational convenience and without confusion, from now on we may write
W^{s,p}
,
H^{s}
and
L^{p}
instead of
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
,
H^{s}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
and
L^{p}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
respectively, for any
p\in[1,\infty]
,
s\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
and
d\in\mathbbm{N}
. In addition, we use the convention under which
C
refers to a positive constant, which may differ from one line to the other. The aim of this subsection is to prove the following lemma, which allows in particular to conclude that the solution
U_{n}^{*}
of
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
is global-in-time.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
T>0
. If
p\geq 2
, then there exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq C,\text{ and }\mathbbm{E}[\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dt]\leq C.

(4.29)

Proof:
Let
T>0
and
t\in[0,T]
, and apply Itō’s lemma to
\|\boldsymbol{\cdot}\|_{H}^{p}
,

\displaystyle d_{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}=

\displaystyle-p\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\Big(U_{n}^{*},AU_{n}^{*}+B^{n}(U_{n}^{*})+\Gamma^{n}U_{n}^{*}+\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big)^{n}+F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})\Big)_{H}dt

\displaystyle+p\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\Big(U_{n}^{*},G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{t}\Big)_{H}+\frac{p(p-2)}{2}\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-4}dt

\displaystyle+\frac{p}{2}\Big\|G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}dt,

(4.30)

where we denote by
\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}
the linear operator such that

\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U^{*}}\Psi=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Psi,\pi_{k}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}})_{H}\pi_{k}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}},\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\Psi\in\mathcal{W},

(4.31)

with
\pi_{k}^{\sigma,U^{*}}=\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*[\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v^{*})]+A(\phi_{k}^{z})\Big]dz^{\prime}
. This implies in particular

\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}dW_{t}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{t}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}.

(4.32)

Step 1:
We compute the contribution of the bounded variation terms first. Remind that, from (
4.4
), there exist two constants
c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle c\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}\leq|(U_{n}^{*},AU_{n}^{*})_{H}|\leq C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt(U_{n}^{*},\Gamma^{n}U_{n}^{*})_{H}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}f(k\times v_{n}^{*})\boldsymbol{\cdot}v_{n}^{*}=0,

(4.33)

since
k\times v_{n}^{*}
and
v_{n}^{*}
are orthogonal. Moreover, by anti-symmetry of
B
,

\displaystyle(U_{n}^{*},B^{n}U_{n}^{*})_{H}=(U_{n}^{*},BU_{n}^{*})_{H}=0.

(4.34)

Also

\displaystyle\Big|\Big(

\displaystyle U_{n}^{*},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big)_{H}\Big|\leq\Big|\Big(g\>\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}{(\beta_{T}T_{n}+\beta_{S}S_{n})dz^{\prime}},v_{n}^{*}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\Big[K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w_{s})\Big]dz^{\prime},v_{n}^{*}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\Big(\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})),v_{n}^{*}\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\Big(g(\beta_{T}T_{n}+\beta_{S}S_{n}),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|+\Big|\Big(K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\Big(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}),\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}+\Big|\Big(K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{H}w_{s})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w_{s}),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|+C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V},

(4.35)

where we used the triangle inequality and the boundary conditions on
v_{n}^{*}
(
2.62
). Notice that the second term on the RHS of equation (
4.35
) can be written as

\displaystyle\Big|\Big(K*(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\Big(u_{s}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}),\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{s}\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}\|\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}\leq C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}.

Therefore, since the left factor in the fourth inner product on the RHS of equation (
4.35
) depends only on stochastic parameters –
u_{s}
and
a_{3}^{K}
– and since
\|w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}\leq\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}
from (
2.11
),

\Big|\Big(U_{n}^{*},\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p\Big)_{H}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}),\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}+C(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H})\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V},

(4.36)

making use of the noise regularity hypothesis (
3.1
). Remind that
a_{3}^{K}
was defined in (
2.46
) as

a^{K}f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}\mathcal{C}_{K}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{K}(\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}f).

Additionally, the following relation holds,

\displaystyle\Big(a_{3}^{K}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}),\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\mathcal{C}_{K}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})),\mathcal{C}_{K}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}))\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big\|K*(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*}))\Big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq 0.

For
F_{\sigma}
, we have

(U_{n}^{*},F_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*}))_{H}=(d_{t}v_{s}+\Big[B(v_{n}^{*},v_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{s})\Big]dt+\Big[Av_{s}+\Gamma v_{s}\Big]dt-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*}),v_{n}^{*})_{H}\\
=(d_{t}v_{s},v_{n}^{*})_{H}+(B(v_{n}^{*},v_{s}),v_{n}^{*})_{H}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{s}),v_{n}^{*})_{H}+(Av_{s}+\Gamma v_{s},v_{n}^{*})_{H}+\frac{1}{2}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{H},

(4.37)

using again the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the boundary conditions (
2.62
). Here the first, third and fourth terms are inner products between parameters and
v_{n}^{*}
, so are bounded by
C\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}
by the noise regularity hypothesis (
3.1
). Similarly, by anti-symmetry,

|(B(v_{n}^{*},v_{s}),v_{n}^{*})_{H}|=|(B(v_{n}^{*},v_{n}^{*}),v_{s})_{H}|\leq\|v_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V}.

Consequently,

(U_{n}^{*},F_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*}))_{H}\leq C\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{H}.

(4.38)

Remark 4.3
.

Considering
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, and using notations of Remark
4.1
, we have by similar arguments,

(v_{n}^{*},\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*}))_{H}\leq|(v_{n}^{*},F_{\sigma}^{BT}(U_{n}^{*}))_{H}|+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}|(\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{s})_{L^{2}}|\\
\leq C\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}.

(4.39)

Hence, the bound on
(v_{n}^{*},\hat{F}_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*}))_{H}
has a similar form.

Step 2:
Now we estimate the contribution of the martingale terms. First,

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}

\displaystyle(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big[\Big(w(v_{n}^{*}),K*[\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})]\Big)_{L^{2}}+\underbrace{(w(v_{n}^{*}),A\phi_{k}^{z})_{L^{2}}}_{\leq C\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}}

\displaystyle-(v_{n}^{*},\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}-\underbrace{\Big(v_{n}^{*},\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{s}+\nabla_{H}\int_{z}^{0}\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w_{s}+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}\Big)_{L^{2}}}_{\leq C\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}}\Big]^{2},

(4.40)

noticing that the second and fourth terms under the sum are directly bounded by
C\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}
since they are inner products between
v_{n}^{*}
and functions of stochastic parameters –
u_{s}
and
\phi_{k}=(\phi_{k}^{H}\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{k}^{z})^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
. Also, the third term under the sum cancels out using that
\phi_{k}
is divergence-free and the boundary condition
\phi_{k}=0
on
\Gamma
. Moreover, denoting
\check{K}:x\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto K(-x)\in\mathbbm{R}
, remark that

\displaystyle\Big(w(v_{n}^{*}),K*[

\displaystyle\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}w(v_{n}^{*})]\Big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}=-\Big(\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}[\phi_{k}(\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*}))],w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}

\displaystyle=-\Big(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}[\nabla_{3}(\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*}))],w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}-\Big((\nabla_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\phi_{k})(\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})),w(v_{n}^{*})\Big)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{H}\phi_{k}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 3})}\|\nabla_{H}\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{H^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}.

Also, by Young’s convolution inequality,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{H}\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\Delta_{H}\check{K})*\int_{z}^{0}v_{n}^{*}dz^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2})}\leq C\|\Delta_{H}\check{K}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H},

and

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{3}\nabla_{H}\check{K}*w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 3})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{H}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\nabla_{3}\nabla_{H}\check{K})*\int_{z}^{0}v_{n}^{*}dz^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{2\times 3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Delta_{H}\nabla_{3}\check{K}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3})}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}.

Thus, gathering the previous estimates,

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}C\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{4})\leq C(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{4}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-4}\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\leq C(\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+1).

(4.41)

Remark 4.4
.

Notice that, upon considering a non divergence-free noise
\phi_{k}
, the third term under the sum in (
4.40
),
(v_{n}^{*},\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{H}
, is not zero anymore. However, if we only consider
p=2
, then the term is cancelled out in the Itō lemma (
4.30
). Moreover, following
[
1
,
2
]
, if we assume that the noise fulfils following the following “parabolicity condition”

\forall\xi\in\mathbbm{R}^{3},\forall(t,x)\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{S},\hskip 9.24994pt\sum_{k}(\phi_{k}(t,x)\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi)^{2}\leq\chi|\xi|^{2},

(4.42)

for some
\chi\in(0,\frac{2}{p-2}\min\{\mu_{v},\nu_{v}\})
– see Assumption 3.1 in
[
1
]
and
[
2
]
– then

\sum_{k}(v_{n}^{*},\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{H}^{2}\leq\chi\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}.

(4.43)

Hence,

\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-4}\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\leq C(\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+1)+\chi\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}.

(4.44)

Therefore, for non divergence-free noises, the previous bound holds when
p=2
, and for all
p\geq 3
such that the parabolicity condition holds. However, taking for example
p=3
leads to
\chi\in(0,2\min\{\mu_{v},\nu_{v}\})
, which can be interpreted as the noise being controlled by the viscosity term – that is to say the noise is (very) small.

Additionally,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big\|G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big\|\mathbf{P}[\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*}+\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{s}+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}]\Big\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big\|\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\Big\|_{H}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s})+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H},\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\Big)_{L^{2}}.

(4.45)

Remark that

\displaystyle\Big\|\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*}+\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{s}+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}\Big\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\phi_{k}\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s}),\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s})\Big)_{L^{2}}+\|A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\Big(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s}),A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}\Big)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(\phi_{k}\phi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}+C\|\phi_{k}\|_{H^{3}}^{2}(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}),

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big\|\mathbf{P}[\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*}+\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}v_{s}+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}]\Big\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{H}+C(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}).

In addition, notice that by Cauchy-Schwarz and Young inequalities, for all
\xi>0
, there exists
C_{\xi}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s})+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H},\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\xi\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{P}[\phi_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3}(v_{n}^{*}+v_{s})]+A\phi_{k}^{H}+\Gamma\phi_{k}^{H}\|_{H}^{2}+C_{\xi}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\xi(\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}+1)+C_{\xi}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\|_{H}^{2}.

Notice that we used the same notation
C_{\xi}
to refer to different constants – in the same fashion as the notation
C
being kept from one line to the other – and it emphasises the
\xi
dependence of
C_{\xi}
. Furthermore, the regularising properties of
K
lead to

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\|_{H}^{2}\leq C(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}),\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}\pi_{k}\|_{V}^{2}\leq C(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}).

(4.46)

Consequently,

\frac{1}{2}\Big\|G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}(a_{3}\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}v_{n}^{*})_{L^{2}}+C_{\xi}(1+\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2})+\xi\|v_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}.

(4.47)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigg(a_{3}

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\begin{pmatrix}v_{n}^{*}\\
w(v_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix},\nabla_{3}\begin{pmatrix}v_{n}^{*}\\
w(v_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix}\bigg)_{L^{2}}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}

\displaystyle+C_{\xi}(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p})+\xi\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}.

(4.48)

Hence, gathering all the previous estimates into equation (
4.30
), we reach

\displaystyle d_{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+

\displaystyle c\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dt+\alpha\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{V}^{2}

(4.49)

\displaystyle\leq C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}(U_{n}^{*},G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{t})_{H}+C_{\xi}(\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+1)dt+\xi\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dt.

Remark 4.5
.

Consider now the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, and use the notations of Remarks
4.1
and
4.2
. The martingale contribution to the energy reads

\frac{p(p-2)}{2}\Big\|\Big[\hat{G}_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-4}+\frac{p}{2}\Big\|\hat{G}_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}.

(4.50)

The first term is estimated using the same arguments as for the problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
. Also, it is straightforward to show that

\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{G}_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigg(a_{BT}\nabla_{3}\begin{pmatrix}v_{n}^{*}\\
w(v_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix},\nabla_{3}\begin{pmatrix}v_{n}^{*}\\
w(v_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix}\bigg)_{L^{2}}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*}\bigg)_{L^{2}}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}+C_{\xi}(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p})+\xi\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}.

Consequently, the covariation term of the transport noise
(\tilde{\phi}_{k}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nabla_{3})\bar{v}_{n}^{*}
is cancelled out by the stochastic diffusion term
\frac{1}{2}(\overline{a_{BC}}\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*},\nabla_{3}\bar{v}_{n}^{*})_{H}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}
. We find the following relation

\displaystyle d_{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+

\displaystyle c\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dt+\alpha\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|w(v_{n}^{*})\|_{V}^{2}

(4.51)

\displaystyle\leq C\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}(U_{n}^{*},\hat{G}_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{t})_{H}+C_{\xi}(\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+1)dt+\xi\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dt.

By time integration and taking the expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\|U_{n}^{*}(t)\|_{H}^{p}]+\mathbbm{E}[\int_{0}^{t}(c-\xi)\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}[\|U_{n}^{*}(0)\|_{H}^{p}]+C_{\xi}\mathbbm{E}[1+\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}dr].

(4.52)

Now use the previous inequality (
4.52
) with
\xi=\frac{c}{2}
, and apply the classical Grönwall lemma, so that for
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\|U_{n}^{*}(t)\|_{H}^{p}]\leq C.

(4.53)

Step 3:
We are now in position to deduce the energy estimates we claimed earlier. Using the inequality (
4.52
),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}(r)\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}(r)\|_{V}^{2}dr\Bigg]\leq C\Bigg(1+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}\Big]dr\Bigg),

(4.54)

and applying (
4.53
) allows to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}(r)\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}(r)\|_{V}^{2}dr]\leq C.

(4.55)

Moreover, by the inequality (
4.49
) with
\xi=\frac{c}{2}
, upon integrating and taking the supremum and the expectation, one also has

\mathbbm{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]\leq\mathbbm{E}[\|U_{n}^{*}(0)\|_{H}^{p}]+C\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{E}[\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]dr+C(T+1)+\frac{c}{2}\mathbbm{E}[\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}(t)\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dr]\\
+\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}(U_{n}^{*},G_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma})_{H}\bigg|\Bigg].

(4.56)

We estimate the martingale term using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (see Theorem 4.36 in
[
16
]
) and Young’s inequality, which yield,

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}(U_{n}^{*},G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma})_{H}\bigg|\Bigg]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2(p-2)}\underbrace{\|[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}_{C(1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{4})}dr\Big)^{1/2}\Big]\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\Big(1+\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2p}dr\Big)^{1/2}\Big]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[1+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p/2}(\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p})^{1/2}\Big]\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}\Big]+C\mathbbm{E}\Big[1+(\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p})\Big]

(4.57)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{E}[1+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]dr,

(4.58)

and by equation (
4.53
), we reach eventually

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}]\leq C.

(4.59)

\square

Remark 4.6
.

1.

If the noise is not divergence-free, then equation (
4.57
) rather reads,

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}(U_{n}^{*},G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma})_{H}\bigg|\Bigg]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\Big(1+\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2p}+\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dr\Big)^{1/2}\Big]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[1+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p/2}\Big(1+\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dr\Big)^{1/2}\Big]\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}\Big]+C\Big(1+\mathbbm{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-2}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{V}^{2}dr\Big]\Big).

(4.60)

Therefore the argument still holds for
p=2
, and so does it for
p\geq 3
if the additional parabolicity condition (
4.42
) is fulfilled.

2.

Adapting the argument to the problem
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
is straightforward, since equation (
4.40
) gives the following bound,

\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p-4}\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})-\mathcal{M}^{\sigma,U_{n}^{*}}\Big]^{*}U_{n}^{*}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\leq C(\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{p}+1).

(4.61)

Hence, Lemma
4.1
holds for both
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
and
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
, with divergence-free noises. Assuming that the (
p
-dependent) parabolicity condition is fulfilled, it also holds for divergent noises.

4.3
Tightness of the laws

Now we show that the solutions
U_{n}^{*}
have tight laws in the space
L^{2}([0,T],H)\cap C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))
. We only consider the “full” problem
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
in this section, since adapting the arguments to
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
is immediate.

4.3.1
Tightness in
L^{2}([0,T],H)

Let
\alpha\in(1/3,1/2)
. In the following, if
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbbm{R}
, we may use the notation
x_{1}\vee x_{2}:=\max(x_{1},x_{2})
. By Theorem 2.1 of
[
22
]
(see also
[
38
]
and
[
53
]
), the embedding

\displaystyle L^{2}\big([0,T],V\big)\cap W^{\alpha,2}\big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\big)\hookrightarrow L^{2}\big([0,T],H\big)\text{ is compact.}

(4.62)

So it is enough to show that
(U_{n}^{*})
is bounded in probability in both spaces
L^{2}([0,T],V)
and
W^{\alpha,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))
.

It can be readily shown that the processes
U_{n}^{*}
are bounded in probability in
L^{2}([0,T],V)
, using Markov inequality and the previous energy estimates. Hence we focus on showing that the processes
(U_{n}^{*})
are bounded in probability in
W^{\alpha,2}\Big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\Big)
, with the inclusion relation

W^{1,2}\Big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\Big)\subset W^{1/2,2}\Big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\Big)\subset W^{\alpha,2}\Big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\Big).

(4.63)

Remind the Galerkin approximation problem
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
,

U_{n}^{*}=P^{n}(U_{0}^{*})-\int_{0}^{t}[AU_{n}^{*}+B^{n}(U_{n}^{*})+\Gamma^{n}U_{n}^{*}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}(\nabla_{H}p)^{n}dt+F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})]dr\\
+\int_{0}^{t}[G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}(\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma})^{n}],

(4.64)

which we rewrite as
U_{n}^{*}:=\sum_{k=1}^{8}J_{n}^{k}
, with

\displaystyle J_{n}^{1}:=P^{n}(U_{0}^{*}),

\displaystyle J_{n}^{2}:=-\int_{0}^{t}AU_{n}^{*}dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{3}:=-\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{4}:=-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma^{n}U_{n}^{*}dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{5}:=-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}(\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p)^{n}dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{6}:=-\int_{0}^{t}F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{7}:=\int_{0}^{t}G_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}

\displaystyle J_{n}^{8}:=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}(\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma})^{n}.

Now we show that each term is bounded in
L^{1}\Big(\Omega,W^{\alpha,2}\Big([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2})\Big)\Big)
, which allows to conclude. Thanks to the energy estimates (
4.29
) and the properties proven in subsection
4.1
, the following holds,

\mathbbm{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{5}\|J_{n}^{i}\|_{W^{1,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))}\Big]\leq C.

(4.65)

For
J_{n}^{6}
, by continuity of
F_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
,

\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|J_{n}^{6}\|_{W^{1,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))}^{2}\Big]=\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|\int_{0}^{t}F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})dr\|_{W^{1,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))}^{2}\Big]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|F_{\sigma}^{n}(U_{n}^{*})\|_{L^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})}^{2}\Big]\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|F_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*})\|_{\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})}^{2}dt\Big]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}dt\Big]\leq C.

(4.66)

For
J_{n}^{7}
, by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (see Lemma 2.1 in
[
22
]
), since
\alpha<1/2
,

\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|J_{n}^{7}\|_{W^{\alpha,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))}^{2}\Big]\leq C_{\alpha}\mathbbm{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|G_{\sigma}U_{n}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R^{3}}),\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma}))}^{2}dt\Big]\\
\leq C_{\alpha}\mathbbm{E}\Big[1+\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}dr\Big]\leq C,

(4.67)

by continuity of
G_{\sigma}\Psi:H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A^{-\gamma})
and using (
4.29
). Furthermore, for
J_{n}^{8}
, since the operator
\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\nabla_{H}p_{t}^{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},H)
is continuous,

\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|J_{n}^{8}\|_{W^{\alpha,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}^{2}\Big]\leq C_{\alpha}\mathbbm{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p^{\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(L^{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R^{3}}),\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}^{2}dt\Big]\\
\leq C_{\alpha}\mathbbm{E}\Big[1+\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{2}dr\Big]\leq C.

(4.68)

Gathering all the previous estimates, we get

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\Big[\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{W^{\alpha,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}\Big]\leq C,

(4.69)

so that
U_{n}^{*}
is bounded in probability in
W^{\alpha,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))
by Markov inequality. Therefore we deduce that
U_{n}^{*}
is tight in
L^{2}([0,T],H)
using that the embedding (
4.62
) is compact.

\square

4.3.2
Tightness in
C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))

Since
\alpha>1/3
, using Theorem 2.2 of
[
22
]
(see also
[
38
]
and
[
53
]
), the embeddings

\displaystyle W^{1,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))

(4.70)

and

\displaystyle W^{\alpha,3}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))

(4.71)

are compact. Using the previous subsection
4.3.1
, we directly have

\displaystyle\Big\|U_{n}^{*}-\int_{0}^{t}\Big[G(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma}\Big]\Big\|_{W^{1,2}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}\leq C.

(4.72)

Moreover,

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}\Big[G_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*})dW_{r}+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma}\Big]\Big\|_{W^{\alpha,3}([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}^{3}\Bigg]\\
\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|\Big[G_{\sigma}(U_{n}^{*})+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp^{\sigma}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}(A^{-2}))}^{3}dr\Bigg]\leq C\Bigg(\mathbbm{E}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\|U_{n}^{*}\|_{H}^{3}\Bigg]+1\Bigg)\leq C,

(4.73)

using the estimate
(
4.29
)
.

Combining these two energy estimates, we conclude that
U_{n}^{*}
is tight in
C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))
, using that the embeddings (
4.70
) and (
4.71
) are compact.

\square

4.4
Taking the limit of the approximate solutions

Now we take the limit of the Galerkin equation to show that limit of
v_{n}^{*}
is a solution of
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
. Again, adapting the arguments to
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{K})
is immediate.

By Prohorov theorem (see Theorem 2.3 of
[
16
]
), the probability measures of
(U_{n}^{*})_{n}
are relatively compact since they are tight. In addition, defining
W_{n}=W
– where
W
is the Wiener process of the stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t},\mathbbm{P},W)
– we deduce that
(U_{n}^{*},W)
is tight in the product space
[L^{2}([0,T],H)\cap C([0,T],\mathcal{D}(A^{-3}))]\times C^{1/4}([0,T],H^{-2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3}))
. This means there exists a subsequence
(U_{\phi(n)}^{*},W_{\phi(n)})_{n}
of
(U_{n}^{*},W_{n})_{n}
such that the associated probability measures converge in the space of probability measures. Consequently, the ordered pair
(U_{\phi(n)}^{*},W_{\phi(n)})_{n}
converges in law in the product space
[L^{\infty}([0,T],H)\cap L^{2}([0,T],V)]\times C^{1/4}([0,T],H^{-2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3}))
. By Skorokhod theorem (see Theorem 2.4 of
[
16
]
), there exists a probability space
(\overline{\Omega},\overline{\mathcal{F}},\overline{\mathbbm{P}})
equipped with cylindrical Wiener processes
(\overline{W}^{n})_{n}
and
\overline{W}
, a sequence
(\overline{U}_{n})_{n}
and a process
\overline{U}
on this space such that

\displaystyle\overline{W}^{n}\overset{a.s}{\longrightarrow}\overline{W},\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{W}^{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{L}}{=}W_{n}=W,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{in $C^{1/4}([0,T],H^{-2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbbm{R}^{3}))$},

(4.74)

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{n}\overset{a.s}{\longrightarrow}\overline{U},\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{U}_{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{L}}{=}U_{\phi(n)}^{*},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{in $L^{\infty}([0,T],H)\cap L^{2}([0,T],V)$}.

Then, denote by
\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t}=\sigma((\overline{U}_{n}(s))_{0\leq s\leq t},(\overline{W}^{n}(s))_{0\leq s\leq t})
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\overline{U}_{n}
and
\overline{W}^{n}
on
[0,T]
. Similarly, define
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t}=\sigma((\overline{U}(s))_{0\leq s\leq t},(\overline{W}(s))_{0\leq s\leq t})
. Thus,
(\overline{\Omega},\overline{\mathcal{F}},\overline{\mathbbm{P}},(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t})_{t},\overline{W}^{n})
is a stochastic basis for all
n
, so that
\overline{U}_{n}
and
\overline{W}^{n}
are adapted to
(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t})_{t}
. Likewise,
(\overline{\Omega},\overline{\mathcal{F}},\overline{\mathbbm{P}},(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t},\overline{W})
is a stochastic basis such that
\overline{U}
and
\overline{W}
are adapted to
(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t}
.

The equality
\overline{U}_{n}\overset{\mathcal{L}}{=}U_{\phi(n)}^{*}
implies, in particular, that
\overline{U}_{n}
is a solution of some projected Galerkin problem
(\mathcal{P}_{\phi(n)})
– see
[
5
]
(Section 4.3.4). Therefore the previously established energy estimates also hold for
\overline{U}_{n}
. Now, we aim to show that the limit
\overline{U}
is a solution of
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
– equation (
3.16
). The solution
\overline{U}_{n}
of
(\mathcal{P}_{K})
fulfils the following equation,

\overline{U}_{n}=P^{n}(\overline{U}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}[A\overline{U}_{n}+B^{n}(\overline{U}_{n})+\Gamma^{n}\overline{U}_{n}+\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p^{\overline{U}_{n}}\Big)^{n}+F_{\sigma}^{n}(\overline{U}_{n})]dr\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\Big[G_{\sigma}^{n}(\overline{U}_{n})d\overline{W}^{n}_{r}-(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma,\overline{U}_{n}})^{n}\Big].

(4.75)

Then, we derive the following variational expression, for all
z\in\mathcal{D}(A^{3})
,

(\overline{U}_{n}-P^{n}(\overline{U}_{n}(0)),z)_{H}+\int_{0}^{t}(A\overline{U}_{n},z)_{H}dr+\int_{0}^{t}(B^{n}(\overline{U}_{n}),z)_{H}dr+\int_{0}^{t}(\Gamma^{n}\overline{U}_{n},z)_{H}dr\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p^{\overline{U}_{n}}\Big)^{n},z\Big)_{H}dr+\int_{0}^{t}(F_{\sigma}^{n}(\overline{U}_{n}),z)_{H}dr=\int_{0}^{t}\Big(G_{\sigma}^{n}(\overline{U}_{n})d\overline{W}^{n}_{r},z\Big)_{H}\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}dp_{r}^{\sigma,\overline{U}_{n}}\Big)^{n},z\Big)_{H},

(4.76)

which we rewrite

J_{n}^{1}+J_{n}^{2}+J_{n}^{3}+J_{n}^{4}+J_{n}^{5}+J_{n}^{6}=J_{n}^{7}+J_{n}^{8},

(4.77)

to show that each term converges to a finite limit. Regarding
J_{1}
,
J_{2}
,
J_{4}
,
J_{5}
and
J_{6}
, it is immediate that,
\overline{\mathbbm{P}}
-almost surely,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{1}\underset{n\longrightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}(\overline{U}-\overline{U}(0),z)_{H},\hskip 9.24994ptJ_{n}^{2}\underset{n\longrightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}(A\overline{U},z)_{H}dr,\hskip 9.24994ptJ_{n}^{4}\underset{n\longrightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}(\Gamma\overline{U},z)_{H}dr,

\displaystyle J_{n}^{5}\underset{n\longrightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{P}\nabla_{H}p^{\overline{U}},z)_{H}dr,\hskip 9.24994ptJ_{n}^{6}\underset{n\longrightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}(F_{\sigma}(\overline{U}),z)_{H}dr.

(4.78)

For
J_{n}^{3}
,

\displaystyle|J_{n}^{3}-\int_{0}^{t}(B(\overline{U}),z)_{H}dr|
